### Cannot predict — what the capture will teach us (9)

- **Does the Windows HID stack put the report-ID byte in the SET_REPORT/GET_REPORT data stage (wLength 1041) or only in wValue (wLength 1040)?**
  - why the binaries cannot answer: The vendor .exe calls HidD_SetFeature with a 1041-byte buffer whose first byte is 0xA0. Everything below that is hidclass.sys/hidusb.sys behaviour and is nowhere in any binary in this corpus.
  - how a capture answers it: Read wLength and the first data byte of the first SET_REPORT. 0x0411 with data 'A0 11' means the ID travels in the data; 0x0410 with data '11 00' means it is stripped.
- **Which bInterfaceNumber carries the vendor collections, and what is the interrupt IN endpoint address on it?**
  - why the binaries cannot answer: The vendor code selects the device by a Windows HID device path plus VID/PID/UsagePage/Usage (cfg107 0x4035f0), never by interface number. macOS ioreg did not record bInterfaceNumber either.
  - how a capture answers it: The configuration descriptor in the enumeration at the head of the capture gives both directly; wIndex on the control transfers confirms which one the tool talks to.
- **What do the 20 passthrough bytes (report 0x1F..0x32) actually contain on a real device?**
  - why the binaries cannot answer: No code in any config tool writes them except by copying the device's own bytes back. There is no default, no UI control, no validation and no name anywhere in the binary.
  - how a capture answers it: The start-up 1041-byte GET_FEATURE response shows them verbatim. Comparing the pre-reset and post-reset values shows whether the device's factory reset clears them.
- **What are payload bytes 0x00, 0x01-0x04 and 0x07 (report 0x10, 0x11-0x14, 0x17) for?**
  - why the binaries cannot answer: 0x01-0x04 are never written at all; 0x00 and 0x07 are written only from the device's own read and are zeroed by the host's default writer. There is no string, control or comparison anywhere that touches them.
  - how a capture answers it: Their values in the start-up read; whether they are non-zero on a used device and zero on a factory-reset one; and whether the device's own post-reset read agrees with the host's zeros.
- **Does the device accept the record, and does it reject a bad one?**
  - why the binaries cannot answer: The host checks only response[1]==1 after a 300 ms sleep. Whether the device validates ranges, ignores unknown bytes, or silently clamps is entirely device-side.
  - how a capture answers it: Compare the record written by an APPLY against the record read back on the next tool launch. Any field the device altered is a device-side clamp; any field it kept is accepted verbatim.
- **What the four unlabelled radio buttons 1062/1067/1068/1069 mean to the device, as opposed to what they do to the record.**
  - why the binaries cannot answer: They have no caption in the resource. Their position (x=8, aligned with the CPI 1..4 rows) and their byte (0..3, factory default 1 = the 800 CPI row) make 'active CPI stage' overwhelmingly likely, but that is layout inference, not derivation.
  - how a capture answers it: Click a radio, then move the mouse and measure the pointer speed, or press the physical DPI button and watch the event-channel report buf[1]==0x02 buf[2] change to match.
- **How dialog 137 (the LED page: 'LED On / Off', 'LED effect', 'Scroll led', 'Logo led', 'DPI led', R/G/B, 'Apply led settings') is reached in the UI, and which record bytes it writes.**
  - why the binaries cannot answer: It is created at cfg107 0x004056bb, not by the four-tab insert at 0x413300, and the APPLY handler harvests only three page objects (main+0xc8, +0x1980, +0x1de0) — the LED page is not among them. I did not trace its creation path to a visible entry point.
  - how a capture answers it: If the page is reachable, changing an LED setting and pressing its 'Apply led settings' button shows immediately which bytes move — the record has spare capacity at 0x1F..0x32 and 0x11..0x14.
- **Whether the hidden buttons 1059 'Apply CPI settings' and 1061 'Surface Calibration' have live handlers.**
  - why the binaries cannot answer: 1059 has a message-map entry (.rdata 0x00557b58 -> 0x0040c1b0) but both controls ship with WS_VISIBLE clear, so no user action can reach them and no capture of normal use will show them.
  - how a capture answers it: It will not, without modifying the process — which CLAUDE.md §1.5 forbids. Recorded so nobody mistakes their absence from the capture for absence of the capability.
- **Real inter-command timing on the device side: how long the mouse actually needs after A0 11 and after A1 13.**
  - why the binaries cannot answer: The 300 ms and 1100 ms figures are host-side Sleep() immediates. They are upper bounds on what the vendor thought necessary, not measurements of the device.
  - how a capture answers it: Timestamps on the control transfers, plus whether the status read ever returns the busy byte 0x03 — which would show the sleeps are not generous.

### Gaps the auditor flagged (5)

- THE BIG ONE - the live-apply frame silently reverts and silently commits, and the plan predicts none of it. 0x00414010, before packing, copies struct2 back into struct1 for four fields: 0x41405b/0x40406f struct2[0x28]->struct1[0x28] (Angle Snapping), 0x414074/0x41407b struct2[0x27]->struct1[0x27] (LOD), 0x414062/0x41408a struct2[0x26]->struct1[0x26] (Disable LED on Lift-Off), 0x414069/0x41409a struct2[0x29]->struct1[0x29] (downshift+smoothing). Its switch on struct1[0x0a] (jump table 0x4143a4 -> 0x4140b6/0x41415b/0x414200/0x4142a5) then reverts the three NON-selected CPI stage records from struct2 and commits only the selected one, plus struct1[0x0a] and [0x0b] into struct2. Consequences that are cheap to capture and that the document should have predicted: (a) a CPI-radio click made while a LOD / Angle Snapping / LED-liftoff / smoothing edit is pending sends the LAST-APPLIED value for that field and DISCARDS the pending edit from the host record; (b) the same click DOES ship pending Polling Rate, Motion Sync, Multiclick, jitter-filter etc. edits, because those live in struct1 and nothing reverts them - so the mouse gets settings the user never pressed APPLY for; (c) editing CPI 2's value and then clicking the CPI 1 radio reverts the CPI 2 edit. For a tool whose design rests on CLAUDE.md 4.1 read-modify-write, 'the vendor's non-APPLY write path both drops and applies edits selectively' is more important than several of the single-byte mappings that were predicted, and it costs one extra capture step (make one page-135 edit, then click a radio, before pressing APPLY).
- The A0 11 status read does not go through the busy-retry wrapper. 0x404180 calls HidD_GetFeature directly at 0x40428c (calll *0x52d1d8) rather than 0x403920, so the resp[1]==0x03 busy loop that busy-status-03 describes (0x40396a-0x4039dc) is unreachable for a write's acknowledgement: a busy answer after an A0 11 is simply not 1, and drives the 3x 300/350/400 ms whole-write retry instead. Worth predicting because it changes what a busy device looks like on the wire after a write versus after a read, and because it tells our own flasher/config core that the vendor does NOT honour its own busy convention on the write path.
- STEP 26 of the capture plan reads the wrong bytes. It says a MIDDLE CLICK reassignment 'lands inside report bytes 0x51..0x57 (the third 7-byte record)'. The packer puts the eight records at wire 0x37+7k, i.e. report 0x47, 0x4E, 0x55, 0x5C, 0x63, 0x6A, 0x71, 0x78; the third record is report 0x55..0x5B. 0x51 is not a record boundary. This is inconsistent with the document's own button-records-8x7 and multiclick-and-spdt-byte (which correctly place record 3's trailing byte at report 0x5B), and an operator following STEP 26 literally would report the wrong window.
- Committed notes config-protocol.md section 12.2 should be corrected in the same commit: its combo table says the LOD combo 1024 holds "'0.7mm'...'1.7mm' in 0.1 steps, then '2.0mm' - twelve". Both fill sites add eleven (0x40bcb5-0x40bd9a in 0x0040bbd0, and 0x40ef2c-0x40f011 in 0x0040ee80); '2.0mm' (0x557f90) is pushed only in the two-item branch at 0x40eef2. Leaving the notes uncorrected while the pre-registration is committed means the next session re-derives from a wrong row.
- A cheap, high-value negative the notes fully support and the plan omits: the 0xC2..0xD9 LOD values can never reach the wire. The APPLY path harvests (0x413f08) before packing (0x413f2f) and overwrites struct[0x27] with the raw index, and the live-apply reverts struct[0x27] from struct2 at 0x414074/0x41407b. So no path exists from 0x0040fa70's table to an A0 11 frame. Stating that as a prediction turns the rival test into a two-sided one - the capture then also confirms that a value the vendor computes and stores is dead - and it is exactly the kind of dead-store our own data tables must not copy.

### Irreversible / one-shot in this area

- FACTORY RESET (step 27) IS IRREVERSIBLE AND MUST BE LAST. Once it runs, the device's original settings record is gone. The 20 bytes at report offsets 0x1F..0x32 in particular can never be reconstructed by us: no vendor code writes them, they have no default, and their meaning is unknown. If the baseline read in STEP 0 is not captured and saved to a file before anything else, that information is lost permanently.
- The tool writes to the device WITHOUT the user pressing APPLY in one case: clicking any of the four CPI-stage radio buttons (1062/1067/1068/1069) or any of the four clickable 'CPI n' labels (1033/1039, 1034/1043, 1035/1052, 1036/1053) sends an A0 11 immediately. Do not click anywhere in the left margin of the CPI rows during the baseline step, or the baseline is no longer a baseline.
- Merely LAUNCHING the tool changes nothing on the device (start-up is A1 02 + A1 12, both reads) — but launching it a second time after a Factory Reset is the only chance to see the device's post-reset state before any host write overwrites it. Do STEP 29 before STEP 28 if you would rather have the device's answer than the host's; doing both in the order given gets both, but only if step 28's frame is captured too.
- Every APPLY writes 115 bytes to whatever storage the mouse keeps settings in. If that is EEPROM or flash with a limited write endurance, this experiment as written performs roughly 40 writes. That is almost certainly fine, but it is worth knowing the number rather than discovering it. Do not loop any step.
- Setting the polling rate to 8000 Hz (step 2) and then moving the mouse will generate ~8000 interrupt transfers per second on the other interface. If the capture is not filtered to the vendor interface this will produce gigabytes and may drop packets. Set it back to 1000 Hz before doing anything that requires moving the pointer.
- Do NOT run any firmware updater during or between these steps. Config tool 1.07 cannot flash — it has no FWFILE resource and none of the updater command bytes appear anywhere in its .text — but a capture that mixes the two would make it impossible to attribute a frame to a tool.
- The mouse is currently at firmware 1.07 and the config tool is 1.07. Do not update the firmware before this capture: the record layout is a property of the firmware as much as of the tool, and a 1.10 device would invalidate every offset in this document without warning.


---

## Unsolicited traffic, the event channel, the third collection

*area key: `config-events-and-third-collection` — 37 written, 11 rejected*


### Pre-registered (26)

#### 1. `event-report-frame` — [D]

**Every unsolicited vendor event is an 8-byte interrupt-IN report whose first byte is 0x03.**

- **Expect:** Wire length exactly 8 bytes. byte[0]=0x03 (report ID), byte[1]=event id, byte[2]=value, bytes[3..7] present but never examined by any vendor tool. Payload is 7 bytes = 56 bits, matching the descriptor's ReportCount 7 x ReportSize 8 at descriptor offsets 125-129.
- **Cite:** [O] interface-A report descriptor bytes 108-131 (0602ff 0901 a101 8503 1901 29ff 1500 2500 9507 7508 8101 c0), .analysis/hidobserve_3367.json; [D] cfg107 0x412c7e movb $0x3,-0xc(%ebp); 0x412ca1 pushl $0x8 (buffer length); 0x412cb3 cmpb $0x3,-0xc(%ebp)
- **REFUTED IF:** A frame whose first byte is 0x03 arrives on the vendor interrupt IN endpoint with any length other than 8, or the FF02/0x01 collection emits reports carrying a different report ID.
- **Matters:** EGGCore's event reader must size its buffer and validate byte[0] exactly as the vendor does; an 8-byte assumption that is wrong silently truncates or misaligns every field.

#### 2. `event-ids-on-wire` — [G]

**Every report-0x03 frame in the capture has byte[1] in {0x02, 0x06, 0xB4} and no other value.**

- **Expect:** Across the whole normal-use capture, the observed set of byte[1] values is a subset of {0x02, 0x06, 0xB4}.
- **Cite:** [G] inferred from the handled set above; absence of a handler is NOT evidence the device does not emit other ids (CLAUDE.md §1.2a)
- **REFUTED IF:** Any report-0x03 frame carries byte[1] outside {0x02, 0x06, 0xB4}. This is the prediction I most expect to be wrong, and being wrong would be a finding: an event id the vendor's own tool discards.
- **Matters:** If the device emits ids the vendor drops, our event reader must log rather than discard them, and the extra ids are free protocol surface no static analysis could ever have reached.

#### 3. `polling-rate-onehot-table` — [D]

**Event 0x06 reports the polling rate as a one-hot byte with bit 0 = 8000 Hz and bit 6 = 125 Hz.**

- **Expect:** byte[2] takes exactly one of seven values, decoding as 0x01=8000 Hz, 0x02=4000 Hz, 0x04=2000 Hz, 0x08=1000 Hz, 0x10=500 Hz, 0x20=250 Hz, 0x40=125 Hz. No other value of byte[2] in 1..0x40 is meaningful: every other slot in cfg107's 64-byte index table routes to the default arm.
- **Cite:** [D] cfg107: bound check 0x413a7d cmpl $0x3f on (byte[2]-1); index byte table at 0x413c04 holds non-default values only at offsets 0,1,3,7,15,31,63 (bytes 00,01,02,03,04,05,06); jump table at 0x413be4 = {0x413b79, 0x413b53, 0x413b2d, 0x413b09, 0x413ae2, 0x413abb, 0x413a94} sending CB_SETCURSEL 6,5,4,3,2,1,0; combo filled at 0x0040bbd0 with '125Hz'..'8000Hz' ascending (config-protocol.md §10.4). INDEPENDENT CONFIRMATION in the other code base: cfg100 0x414544 with jump table at 0x414638, byte[2]=1,2,4,8 -> CB_SETCURSEL 3,2,1,0 against cfg104's DLGINIT list '1000Hz 2000Hz 4000Hz 8000Hz' — the same bit-to-rate mapping, four rates instead of seven.
- **REFUTED IF:** byte[2] of an event-0x06 frame is not one of {1,2,4,8,0x10,0x20,0x40}, or the decoded rate contradicts the measured report-0x01 interval.
- **Matters:** It is the only decoded field of the event channel and the first cross-check between a wire byte and a physically measurable quantity, so it is the cheapest test of whether the whole derivation touches reality.

#### 4. `polling-rate-matches-interval` — [D]

**The rate decoded from event 0x06 equals the actual report-0x01 arrival rate during continuous movement.**

- **Expect:** Median inter-arrival time of consecutive report-0x01 interrupt-IN completions during sustained movement equals 1/rate within 2%: 125 us at 8000 Hz, 250 us at 4000, 500 us at 2000, 1 ms at 1000, 2 ms at 500, 4 ms at 250, 8 ms at 125.
- **Cite:** [O] both interfaces report ReportInterval = 125 (microseconds) in ioreg, so the endpoint is serviced every 125 us and the device throttles by NAKing; [D] the one-hot rate table of `#3 polling-rate-onehot-table` in this section — cfg107 bound check 0x413a7d (`cmpl $0x3f` on byte[2]-1) and the index byte table at 0x413c04. *"the one-hot table above" named no address; corrected 2026-09-06.*
- **REFUTED IF:** The measured interval disagrees with the decoded rate by more than 2%, or the interval is 1 ms regardless of the decoded value — which would mean the byte is not the polling rate at all.
- **Matters:** It validates the event decode end to end using nothing but Wireshark timestamps, with no vendor software and no writes.

#### 5. `cpi-stage-event-range` — [D]

**Event 0x02 carries a plain 0-based index in 0..3, not a mask.**

- **Expect:** byte[2] of an event-0x02 frame is one of 0x00, 0x01, 0x02, 0x03. It is stored verbatim, with no shift and no decrement, and selects radio button index 0..3 in that exact order.
- **Cite:** [D] cfg107 0x40f751 movb %al,0x57f21a (raw store), 0x40f75b cmpl $0x3,%eax with 0x40f75e ja default, jump table at 0x40f8ac = {0x40f76b, 0x40f85b, 0x40f7bb, 0x40f80b} which BM_SETCHECK the radios at obj+0x13f4, +0x1468, +0x14dc, +0x1550 respectively
- **REFUTED IF:** An event-0x02 frame carries byte[2] >= 4, or the observed values are one-hot (1,2,4,8) rather than 0..3.
- **Matters:** Two adjacent fields of the same channel use two different encodings — one-hot for rate, ordinal for this one. Getting that backwards writes a wrong byte into the settings record.

#### 6. `event-crosscheck-polling-rate-blob` — [D]

**The polling-rate byte the device pushes on the event channel is the SAME byte as offset 0x15 of the 1041-byte feature response to A1 12.**

- **Expect:** In the GET_REPORT(Feature, id 0xA0) data stage of 1041 bytes, wire offset 0x15 (payload offset 0x05, since the 1024-byte payload starts at buffer offset 0x10) holds exactly the one-hot byte carried in event 0x06's byte[2]. The same byte sits at wire offset 0x15 of the 1041-byte SET_REPORT data stage of an A0 11 write.
- **Cite:** [D] cfg107 read path: 0x403bce leal -0x468(%ebp) (buffer base), 0x403bf2 leal -0x458(%ebp) (payload = base+0x10), 0x403c04 calll 0x403c40. Unpacker: 0x403c49 movzbl 0x57f345 then 0x403c50 movb %dl,0x6(%eax) with eax=0x57f210 set at 0x403bfd — raw[0x05] -> record[0x06]. Event handler stores the mask into record byte 0x06 at 0x413aac movb %al,0x57f216 and 0x413ab1 movb %al,0x57f2a6. Inverse confirmed in the builder: 0x4042d5 movzbl 0x6(%ecx) then 0x4042d9 movb %dl,0x5(%eax), whose output is copied to buffer+0x10 at 0x404227-0x404233.
- **REFUTED IF:** Wire offset 0x15 of the A0 response does not equal the last event-0x06 byte[2], or an APPLY that changes only the polling rate changes some byte of the A0 11 data stage other than offset 0x15.
- **Matters:** It is a two-channel agreement test on a single byte. If it holds it anchors the settings-record offset map to a value readable two independent ways; if it fails, our belief that the 1024-byte payload starts at buffer offset 0x10 is wrong, and that offset is load-bearing for every config write we will ever make.

#### 7. `event-crosscheck-cpi-blob` — [D]

**The event-0x02 value is the SAME byte as offset 0x1D of the 1041-byte feature response to A1 12.**

- **Expect:** Wire offset 0x1D (payload offset 0x0D) of the A0 GetFeature response equals event 0x02's byte[2], a value in 0..3.
- **Cite:** [D] cfg107 unpacker 0x403c99 movzbl 0x57f34d then 0x403ca0 movb %dl,0xa(%eax) — raw[0x0D] -> record[0x0A]; event handler 0x40f751/0x40f756 store byte[2] into record byte 0x0A (0x57f21a and 0x57f2aa); inverse builder 0x40430d movzbl 0xa(%ecx) then 0x404311 movb %dl,0xd(%eax)
- **REFUTED IF:** Wire offset 0x1D does not track the event-0x02 value, or takes values outside 0..3.
- **Matters:** It pins a second independent offset by the same method, which is what turns one agreement into a pattern rather than a coincidence.

#### 8. `event-payload-tail-zero` — [G]

**Bytes 3 through 7 of every report-0x03 frame are zero.**

- **Expect:** byte[3]=byte[4]=byte[5]=byte[6]=byte[7]=0x00 in every event frame observed.
- **Cite:** [G]. The vendor tool reads only byte[0] (cfg107 0x412cb3), byte[1] (0x412cad) and byte[2] (0x412cd0); bytes 3..7 are touched nowhere in cfg107 or cfg100, so nothing in the binaries constrains them.
- **REFUTED IF:** Any of bytes 3..7 is non-zero in any frame. Cheap to be wrong and worth being wrong: a non-zero tail is five bytes of device state the vendor discards.
- **Matters:** Our reader should log all eight bytes rather than the three the vendor reads; if the tail is genuinely zero we can assert it as an invariant in the mock harness, and if it is not, it is new protocol.

#### 9. `no-idle-polling` — [D]

**With the vendor config tool open and untouched, there is NO periodic host-to-device traffic on this device.**

- **Expect:** Over a 10-minute window with the config tool running and nothing clicked, the capture shows zero control transfers to the device after the initial open, and no repeating pattern at any fixed interval.
- **Cite:** [D] the event reader is a blocking read, not a poll: cfg107 0x412c99-0x412c9e computes ecx = (dev->blocking ? -1 : 0) and passes it as hid_read_timeout's timeout, i.e. hidapi's hid_read; the Sleep(0x50) = 80 ms at 0x412ce4 runs only AFTER a report has arrived. Search space for a timer: all FF15-through-IAT call sites of USER32!SetTimer recovered from raw bytes by calledges.py over every executable section — 28 sites, all at VA >= 0x431972, i.e. all inside statically-linked MFC, none in the application range [0x40bbd0, 0x415000). BLIND SPOT per §1.2a: application code calling CWnd::SetTimer would appear as a call INTO an MFC body, not as an IAT site, and this scan cannot see that.
- **REFUTED IF:** Control transfers repeat at a fixed interval while the tool is idle. If they do, find the caller before concluding our tool may poll.
- **Matters:** It shows the vendor's design is push-only, so our config tool can adopt the same discipline and emit no periodic traffic that could collide with a flash in progress.

#### 10. `attach-burst-small` — [G]

**The device emits a small, bounded number of unsolicited input reports around enumeration, not a burst.**

- **Expect:** Between 0 and 4 interrupt-IN transfers on the keyboard/vendor interface in the 2 seconds after SET_CONFIGURATION completes. If any are report 0x03, I expect at most one event-0x06 frame and at most one event-0x02 frame, announcing the current polling rate and current CPI stage.
- **Cite:** [O] a total of 2 input reports on interface A over a >=929 s attachment (hidobserve_3367.json); [G] for attributing those two to attach-time announcements — the later of them arrived 929 s after the last mouse report, so at least one was NOT at attach, which argues against this prediction as much as for it.
- **REFUTED IF:** More than 4 input reports on the keyboard/vendor interface within 2 s of enumeration, or zero when the device state had clearly changed, or a frame carrying a report ID other than the two predicted.
- **Matters:** If the device announces its state at attach, our tool gets the polling rate and CPI stage for free without sending a single byte — the cheapest conceivable stage-1 read.

#### 11. `event-echo-after-apply` — [G]

**A host-initiated polling-rate change (APPLY -> A0 11) is echoed back by the device as an unsolicited event 0x06.**

- **Expect:** Within 2 s of the SET_REPORT(Feature, 0xA0) whose data-stage byte 0x15 changed, a report-0x03 frame arrives with byte[1]=0x06 and byte[2] equal to the newly written one-hot value.
- **Cite:** [G]. Nothing in either code base establishes this. The handler is idempotent — it only sets a combo selection — so an echo would be harmless but is not required. Committing to a value rather than hedging.
- **REFUTED IF:** No report-0x03 frame follows an APPLY that changed the polling rate, which would mean the channel reports device-originated changes only.
- **Matters:** If the device echoes host writes, an event arriving during our own write sequence is not evidence of a race, and our verify-after-write logic must not treat it as one.

#### 12. `rate-change-no-reenumeration` — [G]

**Changing the polling rate does not re-enumerate the device.**

- **Expect:** No USB disconnect, no re-address and no repeated GET_DESCRIPTOR sequence after an APPLY that changes the polling rate. The endpoint keeps bInterval = 1 and the device simply NAKs more microframes.
- **Cite:** [O] ReportInterval = 125 us on BOTH interfaces in application mode (hidobserve_3367.json) and in bootloader mode (.analysis/ioreg_bootloader_1977_20260904.txt), so the declared service interval is 125 us regardless of the configured rate and a rate change need not touch the descriptor; [G] for the inference that it therefore does not re-enumerate.
- **REFUTED IF:** The capture shows the device drop off the bus and re-enumerate after a polling-rate change.
- **Matters:** Our config tool holds an open handle across a write. If a rate change re-enumerates, that handle dies mid-sequence and the tool must expect it — the same problem the flasher faces at bootloader entry.

#### 13. `col5-negative-needs-positive-control` — [G]

**A capture showing no report 0x08 is meaningful only if the SAME capture shows report 0x03. Report 0x03 is the positive control.**

- **Expect:** Reports 0x03 and 0x08 arrive on the same interrupt IN endpoint of the same interface, because both collections are declared in the single 156-byte descriptor of one interface and that interface has exactly one endpoint. So a capture containing at least one report-0x03 frame proves the endpoint's transfers are being recorded, and only then does the absence of 0x08 say anything about the device rather than about the rig.
- **Cite:** [O] both FF02 collections lie inside the same 156-byte report descriptor (offsets 108-155) on one USB interface, and that interface has bNumEndpoints = 1 in bootloader mode (.analysis/ioreg_bootloader_1977_20260904.txt, IOUSBHostInterface@1). [G] for the Windows-stack claim that HIDCLASS keeps the endpoint read pump running because kbdhid holds the keyboard collection open, so reports for unopened collections still cross the wire and are still captured by USBPcap.
- **REFUTED IF:** The capture contains neither report 0x03 nor report 0x08 — in which case it proves nothing about collection 5 and must be redone, not interpreted.
- **Matters:** Without this rule an empty result gets read as an answer. CLAUDE.md §1.2a exists exactly for this, and this is its concrete form here.

#### 14. `mouse-report-layout` — [O]

**Ordinary mouse motion uses report 0x01 on the mouse interface, 8 bytes, with 16-bit X and Y.**

- **Expect:** byte[0]=0x01; byte[1]=button bitmap where bit N is HID Button (N+1), buttons 1..8; bytes[2:4]=X as int16 little-endian, range -32768..32767; bytes[4:6]=Y as int16 LE; byte[6]=Wheel as int8, -127..127; byte[7]=AC Pan (Consumer usage 0x0238) as int8, -127..127. Seven payload bytes plus the report ID.
- **Cite:** [O] the 69-byte report descriptor, parsed: ReportID 1 at offset 6; Buttons 1..8 as 8 x 1 bit at offsets 12-26; Usage 0x30/0x31 with LogicalMin 0x8000 / LogicalMax 0x7FFF, ReportSize 16, ReportCount 2 at offsets 28-44; Usage 0x38 ReportSize 8 at offsets 46-56; Consumer 0x0238 ReportSize 8 at offsets 58-65. Corroborated by macOS: HIDPointerButtonCount = 8 and a RelativePointer element of ReportSize 16, Min -32768, Max 32767.
- **REFUTED IF:** X or Y appear as 8-bit, or the field order differs, or the frame is not 8 bytes.
- **Matters:** This packet will be the overwhelming majority of the capture, and being able to filter it out precisely is what makes the interesting remainder findable at all.

#### 15. `mouse-silent-when-idle` — [G]

**An untouched mouse produces ZERO interrupt-IN transfers; it NAKs rather than sending null reports.**

- **Expect:** With the mouse stationary and untouched for 60 s, the capture contains no report-0x01 transfers in that window (USBPcap logs completed URBs, and a NAKed IN does not complete).
- **Cite:** [G]. Supporting [O]: macOS counted 54 input reports on the mouse interface over an attachment of at least 929 s, which is inconsistent with any continuous reporting.
- **REFUTED IF:** Report-0x01 frames with an all-zero payload arrive at a constant rate while nothing touches the mouse.
- **Matters:** It sets the expected capture volume. If the device streams at 8 kHz while idle, a 15-minute idle capture is 7.2 million packets and the whole capture plan has to change.

#### 16. `side-button-numbering` — [G]

**The rear side button is HID button 4 (bit 3) and the front side button is HID button 5 (bit 4).**

- **Expect:** Pressing the side button nearer the user sets byte[1] bit 3; the one nearer the front of the mouse sets bit 4.
- **Cite:** [G]. The vendor's own dialog orders the combos Right(1072), Middle(1020), Forward(1021), Back(1022) — Forward BEFORE Back — which is the opposite of the usual convention that button 4 is Back. cfg107 DIALOG 139 via dlgdump.py.
- **REFUTED IF:** The two bits are the other way round. Genuinely 50/50, and the vendor's own control order argues against my prediction, which is precisely why it is written down.
- **Matters:** Button-remap writes must name the right button; getting this backwards writes the user's Back binding onto Forward and vice versa.

#### 17. `acpan-always-zero` — [G]

**byte[7] (AC Pan / tilt wheel) is always 0x00.**

- **Expect:** Across the whole capture, byte[7] of every report-0x01 frame is 0x00.
- **Cite:** [G]. The field is declared (Consumer usage 0x0238, descriptor offsets 58-65) but the config tool exposes no tilt-wheel binding anywhere: DIALOG 139 lists Right, Middle, Forward, Back, Wheel Up, Wheel Down and nothing else (dlgdump.py cfg107).
- **REFUTED IF:** byte[7] is ever non-zero.
- **Matters:** A non-zero value means the wheel tilts and the vendor's UI does not expose it — a physical control the config tool cannot configure, which is the same shape of finding as the unopened third collection.

#### 18. `keyboard-collection-silent-by-default` — [O]

**The boot-keyboard collection carries NO traffic on a factory-default device.**

- **Expect:** Zero 8-byte interrupt-IN frames with byte[0]=0x02 during clicking, scrolling and movement, unless a button has first been bound to a keyboard key. When it does fire the layout is byte[0]=0x02, byte[1]=modifier bitmap (usages 0xE0-0xE7: LCtrl, LShift, LAlt, LGui, RCtrl, RShift, RAlt, RGui), byte[2]=0x00 reserved, bytes[3..7]=five keycode slots — note FIVE, not the usual six.
- **Cite:** [O] descriptor offsets 0-46 (ReportCount 5 x ReportSize 8 for the keycode array at offsets 30-44); [D] the binding UI exists — cfg107 DIALOG 152 'KEYBOARD KEY' with SHIFT/CTRL/WIN/ALT checkboxes, targeted by DIALOG 139's six per-button combos (dlgdump.py)
- **REFUTED IF:** Report-0x02 frames appear during ordinary clicking on an unconfigured device, which would mean some button ships bound to a key.
- **Matters:** This collection is why macOS runs Keyboard Setup Assistant on plug-in. If it also carries traffic by default, we have mis-read what the device ships doing, and our tool's advice to the user about that dialog is wrong.

#### 19. `consumer-collection-silent-by-default` — [O]

**The Consumer Control collection carries NO traffic on a factory-default device, and scrolling does NOT use it.**

- **Expect:** Zero 3-byte interrupt-IN frames with byte[0]=0x06. When it does fire, after a media binding is set, the frame is exactly 3 bytes: byte[0]=0x06 and bytes[1:3] a single 16-bit Consumer usage, little-endian, in 0x0001..0x023C. Scrolling produces report 0x01 byte[6], never report 0x06.
- **Cite:** [O] descriptor offsets 83-107: ReportID 6, UsageMin 1, UsageMax 0x23C, LogicalMin 1, LogicalMax 0x23C, ReportCount 1, ReportSize 16 — a single 16-bit array item; [D] the media bindings exist in the vendor UI ('PLAY/PAUSE' 'NEXT' 'PREVIOUS' 'MUTE' 'VOLUME UP' 'VOLUME DOWN' 'BROWSER' 'EXPLORER', config-protocol.md §12.2)
- **REFUTED IF:** Scrolling emits report 0x06, or report-0x06 frames appear with no media binding configured.
- **Matters:** If scrolling routed through the consumer collection instead of the mouse report, the wheel-remap settings would live somewhere different in the record than we would otherwise assume.

#### 20. `two-interfaces-numbering` — [G]

**The device presents exactly two HID interfaces: number 0 is the mouse, number 1 carries the keyboard and all three vendor collections.**

- **Expect:** bInterfaceNumber 0: class 3, subclass 1, protocol 2 (boot mouse), 1 endpoint, 69-byte report descriptor. bInterfaceNumber 1: class 3, subclass 1, protocol 1 (boot keyboard), 1 endpoint, 156-byte report descriptor. Therefore every SET_REPORT/GET_REPORT for the vendor collections carries wIndex = 1.
- **Cite:** [O] for bootloader mode exactly as stated: .analysis/ioreg_bootloader_1977_20260904.txt, IOUSBHostInterface@0 (bInterfaceNumber 0, bInterfaceProtocol 2, bNumEndpoints 1) and IOUSBHostInterface@1 (bInterfaceNumber 1, bInterfaceProtocol 1, bNumEndpoints 1). [G] that application mode numbers them the same: macOS's application-mode capture came through IOHIDDevice objects, which do not carry bInterfaceNumber, and the registry-ID ordering in hidobserve_3367.json actually lists the 156-byte interface FIRST, which points the other way.
- **REFUTED IF:** In application mode the GET_DESCRIPTOR(Report) for interface 0 returns 156 bytes, or the vendor's SET_REPORT carries wIndex = 0.
- **Matters:** wIndex is a field our transport must fill. hidapi hides it, but any raw-USB fallback or diagnosis of a stalled request needs the right interface number — and I am flagging explicitly that my own evidence is contradictory here.

#### 21. `report-descriptors-byte-exact` — [O]

**The two GET_DESCRIPTOR(Report) responses in the capture are byte-for-byte the descriptors already in hand.**

- **Expect:** Exactly two GET_DESCRIPTOR(Report) control transfers during enumeration, one per interface, wLength 69 and 156. The 69-byte one is 05010902a10185010901a1000509190129081500250195087501810205010930093116008026ff7f75109502810609381581257f950175088106050c0a380295018106c0c0. The 156-byte one is the application-mode hex recorded in notes/device-predictions.md, which carries 0x15 0x01 at offsets 34-35.
- **Cite:** [O] .analysis/hidobserve_3367.json ReportDescriptor properties for both interfaces
- **REFUTED IF:** Any byte differs, or a third report descriptor is requested, or the lengths are not 69 and 156.
- **Matters:** It is the cheapest possible proof that the Windows rig and this Mac are looking at the same device in the same state, and it validates the entire descriptor-derived half of this document in one comparison.

#### 22. `no-interrupt-out-transfers` — [O]

**There are ZERO interrupt OUT transfers in the entire capture. Every host-to-device byte travels on the control pipe.**

- **Expect:** No OUT endpoint on either interface; all A0/A1 traffic is SET_REPORT / GET_REPORT on endpoint 0.
- **Cite:** [O] MaxOutputReportSize = 1 on both interfaces in application mode (hidobserve_3367.json), i.e. no Output items are declared anywhere in either descriptor; [O] bNumEndpoints = 1 on both bootloader interfaces (.analysis/ioreg_bootloader_1977_20260904.txt). BLIND SPOT: bNumEndpoints was read in bootloader mode only; application mode is inferred from the absence of Output items.
- **REFUTED IF:** An OUT endpoint appears in the configuration descriptor, or any bulk or interrupt OUT transfer is captured.
- **Matters:** It settles that our flasher and config tool need only the control pipe, so hidapi's feature-report path is sufficient and no raw-endpoint work is ever required.

#### 23. `feature-reports-are-control-transfers` — [D]

**Every A0/A1 exchange appears as an ep0 control transfer with the report ID as the first data byte.**

- **Expect:** SET_REPORT: bmRequestType 0x21, bRequest 0x09, wValue 0x03A1 (or 0x03A0), wIndex = the vendor interface number, wLength 64 (or 1041); the data stage begins with 0xA1 (or 0xA0) followed by the command byte. GET_REPORT: bmRequestType 0xA1, bRequest 0x01, same wValue, wLength 64 or 1041, data stage beginning with the report ID. The settings read is A1 12 (64 bytes out), then after Sleep(80 ms) a 1041-byte GET_REPORT on 0xA0.
- **Cite:** [O] the descriptor declares 0xA0 and 0xA1 as Feature only (0xB1 0x03 at offsets 66 and 80), never Input or Output, so they cannot travel on an interrupt endpoint; [D] cfg107 0x403b8a movl $0x12a1, 0x403bc9 movl $0x411, 0x403bd4 movb $0xa0, 0x404218 movl $0x11a0, 0x404238 movl $0x411
- **REFUTED IF:** A0/A1 traffic appears on an interrupt endpoint, or the data stage omits the report ID, or wLength is 1040 rather than 1041.
- **Matters:** The 1041-versus-1040 question decides whether our payload offsets are off by one, and every settings-record offset in this document depends on the report ID occupying byte 0 of the data stage.

#### 24. `high-speed-125us-interval` — [O]

**The device enumerates at USB high speed and both interrupt IN endpoints have bInterval = 1 (125 us).**

- **Expect:** bcdUSB 0x0200, bMaxPacketSize0 = 64, operating at 480 Mbit/s; each interface's single interrupt IN endpoint has bInterval = 1, giving a 125 us service interval — which is what makes 8000 Hz reachable at all.
- **Cite:** [O] .analysis/ioreg_bootloader_1977_20260904.txt: USBSpeed = 3, bcdUSB = 512 (0x0200), bMaxPacketSize0 = 64; ReportInterval = 125 on both interfaces in BOTH modes (also hidobserve_3367.json)
- **REFUTED IF:** The capture shows the device at full speed (12 Mbit/s), or bInterval other than 1, or a service interval other than 125 us.
- **Matters:** At full speed the maximum achievable rate would be 1000 Hz and the 8000 Hz entry in the one-hot table would be nonsense — so this falsifies or supports the polling-rate decode by an entirely independent route.

#### 25. `bootloader-usb-identity` — [O]

**Bootloader mode is VID 0x3367 / PID 0x1977 with strings 'EGG' and 'Bootloader' and bcdDevice 0x0006.**

- **Expect:** idVendor 13159 (0x3367), idProduct 6519 (0x1977), iManufacturer -> 'EGG', iProduct -> 'Bootloader', iSerialNumber = 0, bcdDevice = 0x0006 (BCD 0.06 under the same decode the notes establish for the application firmware), bNumConfigurations = 1, bDeviceClass 0, bMaxPacketSize0 64.
- **Cite:** [O] .analysis/ioreg_bootloader_1977_20260904.txt top-level IOUSBHostDevice properties, including UsbDeviceSignature <673377190600000000030102030101>
- **REFUTED IF:** A Windows capture of a bootloader entry shows different strings, a different PID, or a bcdDevice other than 0x0006.
- **Matters:** CLAUDE.md §4.2 requires the flasher to refuse an unrecognised bootloader identity. This is the first concrete identity to recognise, and it came for free instead of costing a stage-2 entry.

#### 26. `string-descriptor-burst-on-tool-start` — [G]

**Starting the vendor config tool produces a burst of GET_DESCRIPTOR(STRING) control transfers to every HID device on the machine, including this one.**

- **Expect:** On config-tool start: one GET_DESCRIPTOR STRING langid (index 0) plus index 1 ('Endgame Gear') and index 2 ('Endgame Gear OP1 8k v2 Gaming Mouse') per HID collection interface enumerated — six for this device (five collections on the keyboard/vendor interface plus one on the mouse interface) if Windows does not serve them from cache. No serial-number request succeeds, since iSerialNumber = 0.
- **Cite:** [D] cfg107 resolves and calls HidD_GetManufacturerString (*0x57f170 at 0x402cf4 and 0x403440), HidD_GetProductString (*0x57f188 at 0x402d29 and 0x403480), HidD_GetSerialNumberString (*0x57f178 at 0x402cbf and 0x4034c0) and HidD_GetIndexedString (*0x57f164 at 0x403504) inside its enumerator 0x402980; [G] for whether Windows serves these from cache or from the wire
- **REFUTED IF:** No string-descriptor transfers accompany the tool's start.
- **Matters:** It is a large, easily-misread block of control traffic that has nothing to do with the protocol; knowing it is coming stops someone reading it as a handshake and deriving a phantom command from it.

### Cannot predict — what the capture will teach us (9)

- **What actually triggers an event-0x06 or event-0x02 frame — a device-side change only, a host-side change echoed back, attach, resume from suspend, or something else.**
  - why the binaries cannot answer: The vendor's software is a pure listener. cfg107 0x412c60 blocks on a read and 0x4139e0 only updates UI; nothing in either code base states or implies a trigger condition. Both config code bases agree, so a second binary adds nothing.
  - how a capture answers it: Timestamp-align the capture against a written log of physical actions. Do each candidate trigger in isolation with 30 s of silence either side: plug in, press the on-board control, change the rate in the vendor UI and APPLY, factory reset, sleep and resume the host. Whichever action is followed by a report-0x03 frame within about 2 s is the trigger.
- **What is in bytes 3..7 of a report-0x03 frame.**
  - why the binaries cannot answer: cfg107 reads buf[0] (0x412cb3), buf[1] (0x412cad) and buf[2] (0x412cd0) and nothing else; cfg100 does the same. Five of the seven payload bytes are read by no vendor code in any version, so no static analysis of any kind can reach them.
  - how a capture answers it: A single captured report-0x03 frame shows all eight bytes. Collect several across different event ids and different values and diff them.
- **The complete set of event ids the device can emit, and what 0xB4 means.**
  - why the binaries cannot answer: The tool handles three ids and silently drops everything else at cfg107 0x412cce jne. An id the vendor chose not to surface leaves no trace. Even the handled 0xB4 case tells us nothing: its value is formatted with L"%d" (string 0x5567b4) into a CStringT that is released without ever being read — the vendor built a display path and then discarded the result.
  - how a capture answers it: A long capture across every state and every physical action, collecting the distinct byte[1] values that actually appear. Any id outside {0x02, 0x06, 0xB4} is new protocol.
- **Whether report 0x08 is ever emitted at all, under what condition, and what it contains.**
  - why the binaries cannot answer: No Endgame binary opens the collection: every hid_open usage filter compares the usage against 1 (cfg107 0x402ec0, cfg104 and cfg101 0x402ec6, cfg100 0x403f26) and neither updater contains a 0xff02 immediate in any form. There is no code to read, so there is nothing to derive. This is the single largest unknown in my area.
  - how a capture answers it: Only a capture can, and only with the positive control in place. To make a negative strong rather than weak it must cover: (a) plug-in and enumeration; (b) 15+ minutes idle; (c) every physical action, including every on-board control and any button held during plug-in; (d) a full vendor config-tool session with every setting changed and applied, plus factory reset; (e) bootloader entry, a complete firmware update and bootloader exit — the collection exists in bootloader mode too, per [O]; (f) a deliberate attempt to solicit it, by opening the Col05 HID path on Windows with a blocking read and by sending each known A1 command while watching the interrupt endpoint. A negative covering only (a)-(c) means very little.
- **Whether the mouse has an on-board control at all, how many, and where.**
  - why the binaries cannot answer: The config tool's remap page names six controls (Right, Middle, Forward, Back, Wheel Up, Wheel Down) and the descriptor declares eight button bits, but neither says anything about a control that is not reported as a button. The existence of a device-pushed CPI-stage event is suggestive and nothing more.
  - how a capture answers it: Press every physical feature of the mouse in turn with the capture running and a written log. Anything producing a report-0x03 frame and no report-0x01 button bit is an on-board control that the HID button map does not cover.
- **The factory-default polling rate and CPI stage of this particular unit.**
  - why the binaries cannot answer: The vendor's host-side defaults (cfg107 0x413db0) are the tool's own initial UI state, not the device's. §7.4 establishes that factory reset is a device-side command with no payload, so the device holds its own defaults and the host never sees them until it reads them back.
  - how a capture answers it: Send A1 13 (factory reset), wait, then read A1 12 and inspect wire bytes 0x15 and 0x1D of the 1041-byte response — or simply watch for the event frames the device emits after the reset.
- **Whether the polling-rate byte can carry more than one bit set, or a value the seven-entry table does not cover.**
  - why the binaries cannot answer: The tool bounds the index to 0..0x3F and routes 57 of the 64 slots to a default arm that does nothing (cfg107 index table 0x413c04). Silently ignoring a value is not evidence the device cannot send it.
  - how a capture answers it: Observe byte[2] across every rate setting, including rates set on the device itself. A multi-bit value would mean the byte is a capability mask in one place and a selection in another.
- **Whether the two input reports macOS counted on the keyboard/vendor interface were event frames, keyboard frames, consumer frames, or report 0x08.**
  - why the binaries cannot answer: macOS's DebugState.InputReportCount is one counter for the whole interface and does not break down by report ID. The IOReportLegend lists channels for report IDs 2, 161, 160, 6, 3 and 8, but those are error counters, not traffic counters, and all read zero.
  - how a capture answers it: A capture logs the report ID of every frame. This is the highest-value 30 seconds of the whole exercise: it is the only direct evidence we will ever have about what the device volunteers when nothing is asking it for anything.
- **Whether Windows' HID class driver keeps the interrupt IN endpoint pumped when no application has the FF02 collections open.**
  - why the binaries cannot answer: It is a property of the Windows HID stack, not of any vendor binary. Nothing in the corpus bears on it.
  - how a capture answers it: Directly: if report-0x03 frames appear in a capture taken with no vendor software running, the pump runs for unopened vendor collections and the same is then true for report 0x08. If they appear only once the config tool is started, then every negative about report 0x08 must be re-taken with a handle open on collection 5.

### Gaps the auditor flagged (5)

- The A0 GetFeature response's byte at wire offset 0x01 must be 0x01. cfg107 0x403be4 `cmpb $0x1,-0x467(%ebp)` rejects the entire 1041-byte settings read unless it holds, and the same structural check gates the A1 13 response at 0x4047e2 (notes/config-protocol.md 4). The document already stakes two predictions on data-stage offsets 0x15 and 0x1D of that identical transfer, so this costs nothing extra in the same capture, and it is the one byte CLAUDE.md 4.1 tells us to mirror as our own structural-plausibility gate. Leaving it unpredicted means the byte that gates every config read we will ever do goes unexamined in a capture that may not be repeatable.
- The application-mode USB identity, predicted the way bootloader-usb-identity predicts the bootloader's. Segments 1-3 are all application mode and none of them has an identity prediction, even though .analysis/hidobserve_3367.json gives ProductID 6520 (0x1978), VersionNumber 263 (bcdDevice 0x0107), Manufacturer 'Endgame Gear', Product 'Endgame Gear OP1 8k v2 Gaming Mouse' and no SerialNumber key on any of the six entries. CLAUDE.md 4.2 requires the flasher to recognise identities on both sides of the mode switch; the string-descriptor prediction even asserts "iSerialNumber = 0" for application mode while citing only the bootloader dump for it.
- That event 0x02's value reaches settings-record byte 0x0A before any bounds check, and what that means if the wire ever carries a value >= 4. cfg107 0x40f751 `movb %al,0x57f21a` and 0x40f756 `movb %al,0x57f2aa` execute before 0x40f75b `cmpl $0x3,%eax`, and the builder at 0x40430d copies record[0x0A] straight into the next A0 11 payload. cpi-stage-event-range predicts the range but not this consequence, and it is the only place in this area where a wire observation feeds a device write - exactly the thing worth writing down before the capture rather than after.
- Whether anything other than report 0x03 is delivered on the FF02/0x01 pipe. notes/config-protocol.md 10.3 records that cfg100 has no buf[0]==3 guard and that cfg101 onward added one - the vendor reacting to something. event-report-frame asserts byte[0] = 0x03 for every event frame but predicts nothing about what else shares that endpoint, even though all four input report ids (0x02, 0x03, 0x06, 0x08) are declared in one 156-byte descriptor on one interface. This bears directly on the document's own headline unknown, and an idle segment could pass with nobody looking for it.
- What the A0 11 SET_REPORT data stage contains outside the fields the builder writes. cfg107 memsets the 1024-byte payload to zero at 0x4041d1-0x4041e7 and then fills only the scattered fields at 0x4042d0 onward, so the vendor's APPLY is built from zero rather than read-modify-write. Segment 3 performs exactly that APPLY, so the capture would settle it for free, and it bears on CLAUDE.md 4.1 and 1.3, which commit us to preserving bytes the vendor apparently does not. This may belong to the config-command area rather than this one, but this document is the one that predicts A0 11 data-stage offsets and prescribes the APPLY.

### Irreversible / one-shot in this area

- THE DEVICE IS ATTACHED TO THIS MAC RIGHT NOW AND IT IS IN BOOTLOADER MODE. Observed 2026-09-04, roughly 23:25-23:31 local, by `ioreg -c IOUSBHostDevice -r -l -w0`, saved verbatim to <repo>/.analysis/ioreg_bootloader_1977_20260904.txt: VID 0x3367, PID 0x1977, iProduct 'Bootloader', iManufacturer 'EGG', bcdDevice 0x0006, two HID interfaces, zero input reports since attach. This session did not put it there and did not touch it — everything run here was read-only ioreg. Establish why it is in the bootloader before anything else happens: it means either a deliberate bootloader entry on the Windows machine or an interrupted flash. In this state there is no working mouse and no event channel. Do not try to 'fix' it from this Mac; the flasher is not built and CLAUDE.md §1.5 forbids running any vendor binary here.
- The attach-time behaviour of the event channel is observable only in a capture that was already running when the device was plugged in. Retrying is cheap (unplug/replug), but the very first attach after a firmware update happens once and cannot be repeated.
- Any experimentation with on-board controls can change the polling rate and the CPI stage persistently — these are settings the device stores, and events 0x06 and 0x02 exist precisely because the device changes them on its own. Capture an A1 12 settings dump (the 1041-byte GET_REPORT on 0xA0) BEFORE touching any on-board control and again afterwards, so the pre-experiment state can be restored.
- Entering the bootloader to capture segment 4 is a CLAUDE.md §4.4 stage-2 action and is not free. It re-enumerates under a different PID and the event channel goes silent for the duration.
- A factory reset (A1 13) run to establish the default polling rate and CPI stage destroys whatever configuration the device currently holds. Capture the settings blob first; it is the only backup that exists.
- Nothing in this area writes to the device. Every prediction here is satisfied by watching. If a capture step appears to require sending a byte to the FF02/0x02 collection, stop — that is a write whose meaning is entirely [G].
