| `cfg-factory-reset-no-confirmation` | `citation-wrong` | The message-map half is right: I read .rdata 0x00558908 as {msg=0x0111, code=0, id=1039, last=1039, sig=57, pfn=0x00413f90}, so Factory Reset's handler is 0x00413f90. But the body citations belong to a different function. 0x00413f90 runs 0x413f90-0x414003 (retl at 0x414003, int3 padding to 0x41400f). The cited "calls the writer 0x00404180 at 0x413f2f" and "EnableWindow(this+0x2e9c, FALSE) at 0x413f6f-0x413f75" are both inside the PRECEDING function 0x00413ea0, which .rdata 0x005588f0 maps to WM_COMMAND id 1043 = APPLY. An exhaustive E8/E9 scan of cfg107 .text gives 0x00404180 exactly two callers, 0x413f2f and 0x414356 -- neither inside 0x413f90. Factory Reset's actual device write is 0x00404720 (exactly one caller, 0x413faf, inside 0x413f90), which builds the A1 13 frame at 0x40479f ('movl $0x13a1,-0x50(%ebp)') and sends via 0x403850. 0x413f90's own EnableWindow(FALSE) is at 0x413ff6-0x413ffc. Only the third sub-citation, refresh 0x0040ee80 at 0x413fd9, is correct. This matters under the brief's "anything feeding a write to the device": the document names the general 115-byte settings writer as the factory-reset path when it is the APPLY path, and the two are different commands. |
| `cfg-some-changes-write-immediately` | `citation-wrong` | Same mislabel, restated: "0x00404180 is one of only two callers-of-record for the write path (the other is 0x413f2f, Factory Reset)". 0x413f2f lies inside 0x00413ea0, the APPLY handler (id 1043, .rdata 0x5588f0), not Factory Reset (id 1039 -> 0x413f90, .rdata 0x558908). Everything else in this prediction replicates exactly: an E8/E9 scan of cfg107 .text finds exactly 8 edges into 0x414010, all jmp, at 0x410cf9, 0x410e29, 0x410f59, 0x411089, 0x4111ca, 0x41130a, 0x41144a, 0x41158a; 0x414010 does call the selector at 0x4130ac-style push $0x1978 / 0x4035f0; and 0x404180 does have exactly two callers. The consequence of the mislabel is that a reader of this document would attribute the immediate-write risk and the factory-reset write to the same function. |
| `upd-progress-values` | `citation-wrong` | The cited arithmetic is exactly right and I verified every instruction: 0x403b18 'movl -0x98(%ebp),%eax'; 0x403b1e 'cltd'; 0x403b1f 'idivl 0x21c(%esi)'; 0x403b2d 'addl $2,%eax'; 0x403b3d SendMessageW PBM_SETPOS; 0x403b43 'pushl $0x5a' (Sleep 90); 0x403b51 'addl $0x64,-0x98(%ebp)'; accumulator zeroed at 0x403a5c; N = 0x21c(%esi) = SizeofResource()>>10 at 0x403242 = 66560>>10 = 65. But the value sequence stated in exact_expectation does not follow from it. floor(100*i/65)+2 for i=0..10 is 2, 3, 5, 6, 8, 9, 11, 12, 14, 15, 17; the prediction states 2, 3, 5, 6, 8, 9, 10, 12, 13, 15, 16 -- wrong at three of the eleven values it commits to (indices 6, 8, 10). Endpoints and count are correct (65 values, first 2, last 100). A capture that showed 11 at the seventh step would be consistent with the binary and inconsistent with the document. |
| `cfg-button-mapping-popup-menu` | `mistagged` | The submenu contents are solidly [D] and I confirmed every one: MOUSE 7 items (0x1f41 'LEFT CLICK' .. 0x1f47 'SCROLL DOWN'), CPI 2 items ('CPI LOOP' 0x55739c, 'FIXED CPI' 0x5573b0, and the GetMenuItemCount<2 guard really exists -- at 0x4087a5 'calll *0x52d864' = GetMenuItemCount, 0x4087ab 'cmpl $0x2', 0x4087ae 'jge'), MEDIA 8 items including BROWSER at 0x55743c and EXPLORER at 0x55744c, DISABLE 0x1f7c; TrackPopupMenu 0x416ce4 with nFlags=2, x=-0x14(%ebp)=rect.left, y=-0x8(%ebp)=rect.bottom. The TOP-LEVEL order and count are not [D]. The three InsertMenuW calls are 0x407232 (uPosition=0, MOUSE), 0x4072b7 (uPosition=0x1f72, CPI), 0x4073b1 (uPosition=3, MEDIA), all with uFlags 0x410 = MF_POPUP\|MF_BYPOSITION. 0x1f72 is a menu-ID constant (it sits between KEYBOARD KEY 0x1f68 and CPI LOOP 0x1f73), used as a BY-POSITION index into a menu that holds two items at that moment. Whether CPI is inserted at all -- and hence whether the top level has five entries in the stated order or four without CPI -- depends on how USER32 treats an out-of-range MF_BYPOSITION index. Nothing in the binary settles that, and the prediction records the 0x1f72 value without noticing it is out of range or hedging the conclusion drawn from it. |
| `irreversible_warnings[3] (item has no id in the document: "one reachable outcome where the flash succeeds and A1 13 is NOT sent")` | `citation-wrong` | The warning says "if the device fails to re-enumerate as 0x1978 within the ~136 s window, the tool reports success and skips the reset (fw110 gate (c) failing at 0x403cc3)", and concludes "'settings were wiped' is not guaranteed either way". The bytes say the opposite. At 0x403cc1 'testl %eax,%eax' / 0x403cc3 'je 0x403e6f'; 0x403e6f pushes 1 and re-enables the button (0x403e81 -> 0x413707), clears the busy flag at 0x403e77, and at 0x403e8c pushes $0x00544ab4 = 'Update failed, try again', SetWindowTextW at 0x403e91. The success text at 0x403e2a/0x403e45 is only on the fall-through at 0x403cc9. So the reachable outcome is: flash completed, A1 13 not sent, tool reports FAILURE. The window is also mis-stated: 0x403c8d 'movl $0x320,%ebx', Sleep(ebx) at 0x403cab, 'addl $0x320,%ebx', 'cmpl $0x3e80,%ebx / jle' gives sleeps 800, 1600, ... 16000 ms, sum 800*210 = 168000 ms, i.e. ~168 s, not ~136 s. Since the document tells the operator this is the case where the on-screen result is ambiguous, and it is in fact the least ambiguous case, this would mislead the reading of the one real flash. |
| `upd-status-string-set` | `citation-wrong` | All thirteen strings are byte-for-byte correct -- I read each one out of the file and every spelling ('send', 'bldr', 'data...', 'Succeed') checks out. The call-site list does not. An exhaustive scan of the fw110 vendor band finds FOURTEEN calls to the SetWindowTextW wrapper 0x004135fa: 0x40322a, 0x40326d, 0x403431, 0x40346a, 0x4034e6, 0x403539, 0x403899, 0x4039ea, 0x403a3c, 0x403ad5, 0x403bbb, 0x403c70, 0x403e45, 0x403e91. The two omitted (push sites 0x40321f and 0x403262) are the only sites for 'Loading firmware file failed' -- which is why that one string's address had to be left as the placeholder "0x544?"; it is 0x005447d0. The list also mixes conventions (0x4034e6 is a call address, the other twelve are push addresses). Separately, neither negative in this prediction carries the §1.2a search-space statement its own claim form requires ("closed set of thirteen", "never shows a modal dialog or message box of any kind"). I checked the substance independently and it holds: SetDlgItemTextW is not in fw110's import table (only SetWindowTextW at 0x51b604), and the band's five SendMessageW sites are WM_ICONERASEBKGND 0x27 (0x403142), PBM_SETPOS (0x403450, 0x403a25, 0x403b3d) and WM_SYSCOMMAND 0x112 (0x403ef3) -- no WM_SETTEXT. |
| `cfg-no-menu-no-tray` | `contradicts-notes` | exact_expectation states "The only dialogs with a caption other than the two main-window ones are 150 'FIXED CPI' ... and 152 'KEYBOARD KEY'". cfg107's RT_DIALOG set is 102, 131, 135, 137, 139, 140, 150, 152, 153, 30721, 30734. DIALOG 30721 (RVA 0x19e734, 232 bytes) is captioned 'New' and DIALOG 30734 (RVA 0x19e81c) is caption-less -- the MFC stock resources that notes/config-protocol.md §12.1 names explicitly as the negative control for the dlgref method ("MFC's own stock DIALOG 30721 ('New') and 30734 score 0 references by the same method in every binary"). The prediction contradicts that inventory without saying so. The substantive claims do hold: rsrc.py cfg107 shows zero RT_MENU and zero RT_ACCELERATOR, and I confirmed 150 (397x137 du, 'FIXED CPI', X/Y checkbox + two edit+slider pairs + OK) and 152 (241x143 du, 'KEYBOARD KEY', SHIFT/CTRL/WIN/ALT + 'Enter a key:' + value static + OK) exactly as described, reached from 0x0040103c ($0x96) and 0x00404beb ($0x98). |
| `cfg-dialog131-never-appears` | `citation-wrong` | Search-space items (a)-(c) replicate exactly and do carry the negative: an objdump -D scan of the whole cfg107 file for the printed immediate $0x83 returns exactly one site, 0x0050db25 'movl $0x83,0x64(%esi)'; rsrc.py cfg107 shows zero RT_MENU and zero RT_ACCELERATOR; DIALOG 131 is at RVA 0x19cb20, 380 bytes, sha256 9660619c44e95a52. Item (d) is not evidence. "cfg107 ships no AFX_DIALOG_LAYOUT for 131, unlike 102/135/139/140/150/152" is true, but rsrc.py shows AFX_DIALOG_LAYOUT also does not exist for 153 -- and DIALOG 153 is created (0x413457 'pushl $0x99' in the page-create block at 0x413300) and shown (TCN_SELCHANGE arm 2 at 0x413cf7). A dialog can be live with no layout resource, so (d) has no discriminating power and should not be listed among the reasons a dialog is unreachable. |
| `upd-flash-timing` | `citation-wrong` | Every Sleep constant checks out -- I enumerated all Sleep call sites in the fw110 vendor band via the KERNEL32!Sleep slot 0x51b214: 3000 at 0x40191e (inside 0x401890), 30 at 0x401d7a, 1000 at 0x403900 and 0x403907, 500 at 0x403a46, 200 at 0x403a9f and 0x403b6a, 90 at 0x403b45, 50 at 0x403bfe, 900 at 0x403dee, and the 0x320-step reconnect ladder at 0x403cab. One parenthetical is backwards: "up to 9 x Sleep(2i) ms during bootloader entry (i=1..9, ~90 ms total if the first read succeeds)". If the first read succeeds only Sleep(2) at 0x4037b3 has run -- 2 ms, not 90. 90 ms is sum(2i, i=1..9), i.e. the total if all nine rounds run, and the loop actually contains two Sleep(2i) calls per round (0x4037b3 and 0x4037ed), so the all-rounds worst case is ~180 ms. Does not change the prediction's coarse 15-40 s envelope, but it is a stated number that the cited code does not produce. |

### Cannot predict — what the capture will teach us (7)

- **Which of the two LOD dropdown shapes this mouse produces — the eleven-item 0.7-1.7mm list or the two-item 1.0mm/2.0mm list.**
  - why the binaries cannot answer: The branch at cfg107 0x0040eeb6 tests a byte the tool copied out of the device's own settings blob into the global 0x0057f23b. Nothing in the binary fixes that byte's value; it is whatever the firmware reports. The default-record writer 0x00413db0 sets a starting value, but 0x0040ee80 runs after the device read has overwritten it.
  - how a capture answers it: One screenshot of the LOD dropdown opened, plus the A1 12 read that preceded it in the USB capture. The item count directly reveals the byte, and the byte's offset in the 1024-byte blob is then pinned by correlating with the record shadow at 0x0057f210.
- **What firmware version FWFILE 140 in updater 1.10 actually is — and therefore the exact number in 'Update Succeed, current firmware version is V…'.**
  - why the binaries cannot answer: The blobs are encrypted (H = 7.94 bits/byte) and the host never decrypts them; the load path at fw110 0x00403200 is a verbatim byte copy. The resource NAME 140 reads as '1.40' under the same BCD convention the tool uses for bcdDevice, but the blob's bytes differ between updaters 1.06, 1.07 and 1.10, which a version-named resource should not do. updater-protocol.md §4 records both readings and refuses to pick one.
  - how a capture answers it: The success line prints the re-enumerated bcdDevice. Whatever number appears there IS the firmware that FWFILE 140 contains, settling §8.5a from outside the binary for the first time. A photograph of that one line is the whole answer.
- **What CPI value each position of the CPI trackbars corresponds to.**
  - why the binaries cannot answer: cfg107 0x0040bde4 sets the slider range to 1..0x578 = 1..1400 in raw units. The multiplier from slider position to CPI is applied where the edit box is written, and that path was not traced in this pass; the four defaults in the default-record writer (0x190/0x320/0x640/0xc80 = 400/800/1600/3200) suggest a step of 50 but that is arithmetic on four numbers, not a derivation.
  - how a capture answers it: One screenshot of the Basic tab with the sliders at their extremes, reading the numbers in the four CPI edit boxes. Slider minimum and maximum against the displayed CPI gives the mapping in one shot, and the maximum CPI is a hard clamp our own writes must respect.
- **Whether Windows itself interposes anything — a driver-install balloon, SmartScreen, a UAC prompt, or an 'unknown keyboard' wizard — when the mouse is plugged in or when either .exe is launched.**
  - why the binaries cannot answer: Nothing in the vendor code produces these; they are shell and PnP behaviour. Neither binary is signed in a way we have checked, and neither requests elevation in its manifest beyond the default (the manifest is 607 bytes, the stock MFC comctl32 dependency).
  - how a capture answers it: Screenshots of the first launch of each .exe on a clean machine and of the first plug-in of the mouse. It matters because macOS pops Keyboard Setup Assistant for the same device (notes/device-predictions.md), and knowing whether Windows does something analogous tells us whether the boot-keyboard collection is genuinely odd or just unfamiliar to macOS.
- **What the four empty SS_NOTIFY statics on the Basic page (ids 1039, 1043, 1052, 1053) display.**
  - why the binaries cannot answer: They ship with no caption and are only 15x8 dialog units. They share BN_CLICKED handlers with the 'CPI 1'..'CPI 4' labels, so they are clickable, but no SetWindowText call targeting them was located in this pass.
  - how a capture answers it: A screenshot of the Basic tab shows whatever text they carry at run time. If they are blank, they are decorative; if they carry a number, they are a fifth readout of the CPI stage values.
- **Whether the config tool and the updater can be run at the same time, and what the UI does if they are.**
  - why the binaries cannot answer: cfg107 serialises its own device access inside a critical section at 0x00578c08 (config-protocol.md §8.1), but two processes cannot share it. Nothing in either binary detects the other; there is no named mutex check in either startup path that was found. What the device does when two hosts hold the vendor collection open is a device question.
  - how a capture answers it: Open both, and press Update while the config tool is polling its event channel. This is already recorded as a proposed CLAUDE.md §5 addition (working-memory 'Flagged for decision #3'); the capture would settle it, but note it costs a flash, so it should be folded into the one real flash rather than run separately.
- **How the tools look at scaling factors other than 100%, and whether the fixed-pixel layouts break.**
  - why the binaries cannot answer: Neither binary declares DPI awareness in the fragment of the manifest we parsed, and both use fixed dialog-unit layouts with empty AFX_DIALOG_LAYOUT resources. Whether Windows bitmap-scales them or they render at native size with clipped text is a shell behaviour.
  - how a capture answers it: One screenshot of each at 100% and one at 150%. Only worth taking if the primary screenshots look wrong, but the answer determines whether any 'control X is missing' observation is real or a clipping artefact — which matters directly for the hidden-control predictions above.

### Gaps the auditor flagged (5)

- Enter and Esc close the updater. fw110's dialog message map runs .rdata 0x544740-0x54479f and holds exactly four entries -- WM_PAINT 0x0f -> 0x403100, WM_QUERYDRAGICON 0x37 -> 0x4031f0, WM_COMMAND id 1001 -> 0x4033b0, WM_LBUTTONDOWN 0x201 -> 0x403ee0 -- with no override for IDOK (1) or IDCANCEL (2); and control 1001's style is 0x50010000, whose low word is 0 = BS_PUSHBUTTON, not BS_DEFPUSHBUTTON. So MFC's stock CDialog::OnOK/OnCancel run and either keystroke ends the dialog. The document's irreversible_warnings say "Closing the window will not stop it", but nothing predicts that two keystrokes close it, and capture_requirements has the operator sweep Alt+letters and F1 on the config tool with no equivalent instruction (or prohibition) for the updater. During the one real flash that is the difference between a recorded observation and a lost capture.
- What the status line says when the post-flash re-enumeration fails, and that the tool is then re-armed. The branch at 0x403cc3 lands on 0x403e6f, which clears the busy flag (0x403e77), re-enables the button, and shows 'Update failed, try again' (0x00544ab4). That is directly predictable and directly contradicts irreversible_warnings item 3; more usefully, it is the one failure arm that does NOT wedge the way upd-wedge-after-device-not-found does, because the flag is cleared inside the worker thread and never re-set. A prediction pairing the two would make the whole busy-flag model falsifiable in one run.
- The per-block retry signature. At 0x403a70-0x403ab1 each 1024-byte block is attempted up to five times (counter at -0x94(%ebp), 'cmpl $0x5' at 0x403aae) with Sleep(200) at 0x403a9f between attempts, before the loop gives up to 'send block data request failed' at 0x403ad0. On screen that is a ~1 s stall at one progress value with the bar never moving backwards. Nothing in the document predicts it, yet it is the visible signature of exactly the post-erase retry policy CLAUDE.md §4.2 requires our flasher to mirror, and the single real flash is the only chance to see whether the device ever actually needs a retry.
- The config tool's background event traffic. notes/config-protocol.md §10.2-§10.3 records a poll thread on a second HID collection started at 0x00412d00. capture_requirements has the USB capture running while the operator clicks a CPI radio button to test cfg-some-changes-write-immediately, but no prediction states what the capture looks like with nobody touching anything. Without a predicted baseline, "traffic appeared when I clicked" is not a decidable observation, and that test is the one that distinguishes the live-apply path 0x00414010 from the deferred APPLY path.
- Whether the CPI submenu is present at all. Given the out-of-range MF_BYPOSITION index at 0x4072b7 (see the cfg-button-mapping-popup-menu finding), the document should have predicted the four-entry outcome as the alternative and named which observation separates them, because one screenshot of the open menu settles it for free and it changes the enumerated domain of every per-button field in the settings record.

### Irreversible / one-shot in this area

- The status line 'Mouse firmware current version 1.07' can be observed exactly once. The mouse is on firmware 1.07 now; after a successful update it is not, and no tool we have puts that number back on screen. Capture it, in the video, before anything else in the flash sequence.
- A successful update ends by sending A1 13, which is factory reset — the same 64-byte frame the config tool's Factory Reset button sends (fw110 0x403dcf and cfg107 0x40479f build it with identical bytes). The user's settings are gone after a normal completion. Capture the settings blob first.
- There is one reachable outcome where the flash succeeds and A1 13 is NOT sent — if the device fails to re-enumerate as 0x1978 within the ~136 s window, the tool reports success and skips the reset (fw110 gate (c) failing at 0x403cc3). So 'settings were wiped' is not guaranteed either way, and the capture is the only record of which happened.
- Pressing 'Update Firmware' with the mouse in application mode commits to the whole sequence. There is no cancel: the updater has no Cancel button, no cancel string anywhere in its band, and the flash runs on a worker thread that the UI cannot stop. Closing the window will not stop it.
- Once the device enters the bootloader (PID 0x1977) it stays there until an image completes. That state is survivable — the updater will flash a device it finds already at 0x1977 with no handshake — but it is not a state to enter casually while experimenting.
- Pressing 'Factory Reset' in the config tool wipes settings immediately, with no confirmation prompt and no visible acknowledgement.
- Clicking a CPI-stage radio button or a 'CPI n' label on the Basic page writes to the device immediately, without APPLY. Treat every click on that page as a write, not as a UI experiment.
- Choosing an item from the Button Mapping popup menu changes a binding. Whether that writes immediately or only on APPLY was not established for those six handlers; assume it writes.
- Downgrading is not established. FWFILE 140 differs between updaters 1.06, 1.07 and 1.10, so an older updater may or may not restore 1.07, and nothing in the binaries says whether the bootloader accepts an older image. Do not treat 'we can flash back' as available.


---

# The capture plan

Collected from every area's `capture_requirements`. Ordering is ours, not
theirs: reversible first, irreversible last, and nothing runs without capture
already recording (CLAUDE.md §1.5 as amended — an uncaptured run of an
irreversible step throws away the observation).


### Updater 1.10 — launch, enumeration, version decode, device-absent

- Confirm the exe first: on the Windows machine run `certutil -hashfile "Endgame Gear OP1 8k v2 Firmware Updater 1.10.exe" SHA256` and check it against notes/binaries.md. Every VA cited here is meaningless against a different file.
- The Endgame Gear Configuration Tool MUST NOT be running (check Task Manager, the tray, and any auto-start entry). It polls the 0xFF02/0x01 collection for an 8-byte input report every 80 ms and that traffic is not the updater's.
- Nothing else may hold the OP1 open. Close any other mouse software.
- USBPcap on the root hub the OP1 is attached to. The mouse is 8 kHz — DO NOT MOVE IT during the capture. Drive the updater's window with the keyboard (Tab/Space/Alt+F4) or a second pointing device, so interrupt-IN traffic stays near zero and the (predicted empty) control-transfer channel is readable.
- Run 1 — free and reversible: start USBPcap, launch the updater, screenshot the window IMMEDIATELY (title bar, status box contents, progress bar, button caption and enabled state), wait 60 s doing nothing, screenshot again, then close via the title-bar X. DO NOT PRESS 'Update Firmware'. Stop the capture. The deliverable of this run is the two screenshots and a packet count, not a protocol trace.
- During Run 1 also record from Process Explorer: the updater's Handles column at launch and just before close, and whether any \Device\HID handle appears in its handle list (Ctrl+H).
- Optional but high value in Run 1: Process Monitor filtered to the updater's process name, Operation is CreateFile — expect ZERO \\?\HID# paths before the button is pressed.
- Run 2 — also free, but ONLY with the mouse PHYSICALLY UNPLUGGED from every port, verified in Device Manager (no VID_3367 device present). Launch the updater, press 'Update Firmware' once, and time with a stopwatch from click to the status text appearing. Then press 'Update Firmware' a second time and record that nothing happens. Screenshot the status text verbatim. Keep USBPcap and Process Monitor running for this one too — Process Monitor gives the 15-pass / 9-vs-6-PID count.
- Record the Windows locale (Region > Number format) so a comma decimal separator in any '%.2f' string is not mistaken for a decode error.
- Do NOT attempt to observe the version string in Run 1 or Run 2. It is only reachable by starting a real flash. If the current version is wanted for the record, read it without risk from Device Manager > the OP1 > Details > Hardware Ids, where it appears as REV_0107.
- If a flash is later performed deliberately, capture the settings blob first (an A1 12 read) — a completed flash sends A1 13 (factory reset) and wipes settings.

### Updater 1.10 — bootloader entry and re-enumeration

- Run 'Endgame Gear OP1 8k v2 Firmware Updater 1.10.exe' with sha256 97cd23d8294461b412bcd02f4a2cadc23fb255dbea874d641d324729aa814144. Every address cited above is a VA in that exact file. Record the sha256 of the .exe actually run; if it differs, the checksum prediction (7D D5 81 00) does not apply and must be recomputed from that build's FWFILE 140.
- Start from APPLICATION mode. The mouse must enumerate at PID 0x1978 before the updater is launched. If it is already at PID 0x1977 the tool takes FUN_004033b0's mode==2 branch (0x403500–0x403513), jumps straight into FUN_00403960, and sends no A1 3A at all — the entire area under test is skipped.
- Capture with USBPcap on the ROOT HUB the mouse is attached to, with ALL devices on that hub selected. Do NOT use USBPcap's per-device filter: the bootloader is a NEW USB device with a new address, and a device-scoped filter silently drops the whole re-enumeration and the flash, making the capture look as though the tool did nothing.
- Plug the mouse DIRECTLY into a port on the laptop, not through an external hub. Behind a hub you additionally get hub-class port traffic (GET_PORT_STATUS, SET_PORT_FEATURE(PORT_RESET), CLEAR_PORT_FEATURE) which is easy to misread as device traffic. If a hub is unavoidable, record that fact with the capture.
- Raise USBPcap's snapshot length to at least 2048 bytes and its buffer to at least 8 MB. The A0 commands are 1041-byte control transfers; a truncated data stage makes the A0 03 checksum prediction — the most valuable single line here — untestable.
- Start the capture BEFORE launching the updater and keep it running for at least 60 s after the success message. The final A1 13 factory-reset frame is sent about 900 ms after the device returns to PID 0x1978; stopping early loses the tail.
- Do not move the mouse during the capture. It polls at 125 us (ReportInterval = 125 [O]); motion generates roughly 8000 interrupt-IN completions per second, which corrupts nothing but buries the vendor commands. An idle mouse NAKs and produces almost no URB completions.
- Note the capture-relative timestamps of the button click, the appearance of each status-line message, and the success message. Several predictions here are timing predictions (the 2000 ms floor, the 3000 ms A0 03 gap, the 22.5 s abandonment) and the status text is what discriminates the two internal failure paths.
- Screenshot the updater window before pressing Update. It should read 'Mouse firmware current version 1.07'. That confirms the bcdDevice BCD decode from the Windows side and confirms the device is a genuine update target.
- Do NOT press Update a second time while the first run is in flight, and do not close the updater window between the A1 3A and the first A0 03. That roughly two-second window is the only moment a kill would leave the device in bootloader mode with the application intact, and that is a separate experiment with its own risks.
- Export the capture as uncompressed pcapng and keep it whole. Do not pre-filter it to 'the interesting packets' before it is compared against this file — several predictions here are about what is ABSENT (no SET_IDLE, no output reports, no probe before A0 03, no busy poll, nothing on report id 0x08), and a filtered capture cannot test an absence.
- If possible take a SECOND capture of an identical run. The MS-OS-descriptor prediction (wValue 0x03EE on first attach only) is only testable with two runs, and a second run gives an independent read on the timing floors and on the path-A / path-B fork.

### Updater 1.10 — the start command, its computed arguments, the 3 s wait

- Run the exact binary at the repo root, 'Endgame Gear OP1 8k v2 Firmware Updater 1.10.exe'. Copy it to the Windows machine and record its SHA-256 there before running, so the capture is tied to the file these predictions were derived from. Do not download a fresh copy from Endgame — a re-cut 1.10 would silently invalidate the checksum prediction.
- Start the USBPcap capture BEFORE the updater is launched, and before the mouse is plugged in if possible. The application-mode A1 3A frame, the 0x1978 detach, the 0x1977 attach and its full enumeration all precede A0 03 and are needed to locate and interpret it.
- Filter by ROOT HUB, not by device. USBPcap attaches to a root hub; the mouse changes its USB address and product id mid-capture, so any per-device filter will lose exactly the transition this area is about. Select the root hub the mouse is on and accept the extra traffic.
- Do not deselect control transfers or restrict to interrupt endpoints. Every byte in this area travels on endpoint 0 as SET_REPORT / GET_REPORT. USBPcap's default captures everything on the chosen hub — leave it that way.
- Leave the mouse in the same physical port for the whole capture and do not touch the cable. Moving it mid-run changes the root hub and truncates the capture at the worst possible moment.
- Do NOT run the Endgame configuration tool at any point during the capture. It shares the transport and the same collection, so its A1 12 / A0 11 / A1 02 / A1 13 traffic would interleave with the flasher's and is easy to misattribute.
- Let the run go to completion. Do not close the updater window, do not cancel, do not unplug. Capture through the device's return to PID 0x1978 and the trailing A1 13 frame, so the A1 08 checksum echo is in the file.
- Record wall-clock time at capture start and keep microsecond-resolution pcapng timestamps (Wireshark's default). The three-second-wait prediction is scored on inter-frame deltas; do not export to a format that rounds them.
- Note the firmware version the updater displays before the run and after it. The mouse was observed at bcdDevice 0x0107 = 1.07 on 2026-09-04, so this is a genuine upgrade; if it already reads 1.10 the tool will still flash (it never gates on version) and the capture is still valid, but say which it was.
- Save the raw pcapng unmodified alongside any filtered export. Scoring needs the SETUP packets, not just the reassembled report payloads.
- Record whether the run succeeded or failed, and if it failed, which of the vendor's thirteen status strings appeared verbatim. 'send bldr start request failed' specifically means the A0 03 five-attempt retry path was exercised, which is a different and equally useful scoring case.

### Updater 1.10 — the block write loop

- Run EXACTLY 'Endgame Gear OP1 8k v2 Firmware Updater 1.10.exe' — the file at the repo root, whose FWFILE/140 has SHA-256 8148ebe9f8d2848abe483aee98df6e42bab341c6a17523bfef0f85f1f66754d0. Copy that same file to the Windows machine and verify its hash there. If a different updater version is run, the payload-byte and checksum predictions must be checked against the alternate values given in 'image-is-fwfile-140-verbatim' and 'start-command-a0-03-declares-count-and-checksum' — a mismatch against 1.10's numbers is then NOT a refutation.
- Start the USBPcap capture BEFORE launching the updater and stop it AFTER the success dialog. Save the raw pcapng; the payload predictions need byte-level data, not a Wireshark screenshot.
- Capture the whole USB root hub / controller the mouse is plugged into, NOT a single filtered device. The mouse disconnects and re-enumerates as PID 0x1977 mid-session, so a per-device filter loses the entire block write loop — the point of the exercise.
- The capture must include the bootloader's enumeration: GET_DESCRIPTOR(Device), GET_DESCRIPTOR(Configuration) and the HID class GET_DESCRIPTOR(Report) (bRequest 0x06, wValue 0x2200) for PID 0x1977. Dump those descriptor bytes verbatim — see the first 'cannot predict' item; it is the highest-value thing here.
- Close the Endgame configuration tool and any other software that opens the device before flashing. Only the updater should hold the collection.
- Plug the mouse directly into a laptop port, not through a hub, dock or KVM, so USBPcap sees a single clean device tree.
- Avoid moving the mouse during the flash, so 260 control transfers are not buried under interrupt IN traffic.
- BEFORE flashing, with the capture running, use the configuration tool once to read the settings blob (A1 12 request, then a 1041-byte GET on report 0xA0) and save those 1041 bytes to a file. It is the only backup of the current settings and the flash will destroy them.
- Record in writing alongside the capture: (a) the firmware version the tool displays before and after; (b) total elapsed time from clicking Update to the success message; (c) how many times the device disconnected and roughly when; (d) what the progress bar did — smooth or stalling, and where; (e) whether any retry or failure message appeared; (f) whether the mouse cursor kept working during the flash.
- If the tool fails at any point, DO NOT re-run it blindly and DO NOT unplug. Stop the capture, save it, and report the exact failure string. The seven it can print are 'send bldr request failed', 'Open bldr device request failed', 'send bldr start request failed', 'send block data request failed', 'get all check sum error', 'send bldr complete request failed', 'Update failed, try again' — which one appeared localises the failure to a specific gate.
- Do not install or run any third-party mouse configuration software on the Windows machine during the capture window. CLAUDE.md §1.1 applies on that machine too.

### Updater 1.10 — read-back verification and the repair loop

- The tool run MUST be 'Endgame Gear OP1 8k v2 Firmware Updater 1.10.exe' (sha256 in notes/binaries.md), started from the Update button, single run. Every per-block byte value in the predictions above — the checksum table, the 0x0081D57D whole-image sum, the identical-block run 0x51..0x73, the byte[1] census — is specific to that binary's FWFILE 140. If a different updater version is run, predictions readback-checksum-exact-values, whole-image-checksum-frame-and-value and readback-payload-entropy-discriminator are void; the structural ones still hold. (Whole-image sums for the others, if it matters: 1.04 = 0x0081F627, 1.06 = 0x0081F40B, 1.07 = 0x0081D408.)
- USBPcap must be filtered on the ROOT HUB / whole USB controller the mouse is attached to, NOT on a single device. The mouse re-enumerates as a different USB device (PID 0x1978 -> 0x1977) when it enters the bootloader, and a device-scoped filter loses the entire flash — every A0 06 and A0 07 in this document happens on the 0x1977 device.
- Snapshot length must be unlimited (Wireshark capture option 'snaplen' = 0 / 'default' turned off). The A0 06 and A0 07 transfers carry 1041-byte data stages; a truncating snaplen makes the read-back-vs-write byte comparison — the single most valuable check here — impossible to perform after the fact.
- Start the capture BEFORE launching the updater and stop it at least 60 s after the success dialog. The tool's post-flash re-enumeration wait can run up to ~136 s (sleeps 800,1600,…,16000 ms) and the A1 13 factory reset is sent after it.
- Do not touch the mouse during the run: no clicks, no button held, no unplug, no moving it to another port. Note explicitly in the log whether it was ever unplugged or replugged, how many times it disconnected and roughly when, and the total elapsed wall-clock time.
- Watch and record the progress bar: the first value shown, whether it reaches exactly 100, and where it pauses. Record the exact text of every status line and of any error dialog, verbatim.
- Run the CONFIG tool once before the update and capture that traffic too — an A1 12 request followed by a 1041-byte GetFeature of report 0xA0 returning the 1024-byte settings blob. The update ends with A1 13 (factory reset), so this is the only chance to preserve the pre-update settings.
- Do not have the config tool open at the same time as the updater. Whether the device tolerates two openers is untested (working-memory 'Flagged for decision #3').
- Save the .pcapng unmodified and keep it; also export a plain-text or JSON dissection if convenient. Do not re-run the updater before the first capture file is safely off the Windows machine.
- Record the mouse's USB address and, if possible, capture on a root hub with no other HID devices, so the trace can be read without filtering ambiguity.
- Make sure the trace includes the GET_DESCRIPTOR traffic for the bootloader device (PID 0x1977) — its report descriptor is currently unknown and this capture is the only free chance to obtain it.

### Updater 1.10 — completion, the post-flash command, final state

- Run the capture against updater 1.10 specifically, and record the sha256 of the .exe used. The checksum prediction 0x0081D57D is the additive sum of FWFILE/140 in the 1.10 build only; 1.04/1.06/1.07 ship different blob contents in the same resource slot and would produce a different number.
- USBPcap must be attached to the whole root hub / host controller the mouse is plugged into — NOT filtered to one device instance, address or PID. The device changes identity twice during a run (0x1978 -> 0x1977 -> 0x1978) and each identity is a separate device instance. A filter bound to the 0x1978 device loses the entire bootloader phase; a filter bound to 0x1977 loses the final A1 13.
- Start the capture BEFORE launching the updater and leave it running for at least 60 s after the success message appears, so the 'no third re-enumeration' prediction is testable.
- Do not export only HID class traffic. The standard enumeration control transfers are the point for two predictions: the bootloader's descriptors at PID 0x1977, and the re-attached device's bcdDevice. Keep the full pcapng, not a filtered export.
- The config tool must NOT be running while the updater runs. It opens the same UsagePage 0xFF01 / Usage 0x02 collection and its own commands would interleave into the capture.
- Settings experiment, in this order, with the capture running throughout: (1) config tool -> Factory Reset -> read blob = D_107; (2) set at least three distinctive non-default values (e.g. polling rate, a DPI stage, a remapped side button) -> APPLY -> read blob = S; (3) close the config tool; (4) run the updater to completion; (5) reopen the config tool -> read blob = P; (6) Factory Reset -> read blob = D_110. Each 'blob' is the 1024 payload bytes of the 1041-byte GET_REPORT reply to the A1 12 request, extractable from the capture itself.
- Run the updater a SECOND time on the already-updated device with the capture still running. That is the only way to test the 'no version gate / identical second run' prediction, and it is the cheapest way to get a second sample of the bootloader's descriptors.
- Record wall-clock timestamps for: the click on 'Update Firmware', the moment the progress bar reaches 100, and the moment the success text appears. Also note whether the mouse cursor stalls at any point and for how long.
- Note the exact text of the final status line verbatim, including the version number and its decimal places, and the text shown before the flash starts.
- If Wireshark is used to read the capture, the fields carrying these predictions are usb.bmRequestType, usb.setup.bRequest (0x09 SET_REPORT / 0x01 GET_REPORT), usb.setup.wValue (expect 0x03A0 / 0x03A1), usb.setup.wIndex, usb.setup.wLength (expect 0x0040 / 0x0411) and the data stage bytes.
- Do not put the machine to sleep, do not unplug anything, and do not press mouse buttons during the flash.

### Which firmware bytes actually reach the wire

- RECORD THE .EXE IDENTITY FIRST. Before running anything, compute the SHA-256 of the exact updater .exe on the Windows machine and write it into the capture notes. Every payload prediction here is keyed to it: 1.10 = 97cd23d8294461b412bcd02f4a2cadc23fb255dbea874d641d324729aa814144; 1.07 = d355346d2abefff094188cef927588c1fc7fa4f209a151f6684d57d189ba98e0; 1.06 = 221c1c49b6ee55a91a5fb12c0690031654035018875207d5f4886649af40a05d; 1.04 = 0fd2016ff89e0ee4bce0d7eedc9b90ca696189090a15ce09ace4570fe8380f9f. If it matches none of these, this whole document is untestable against that run.
- USE THE ROOT-HUB FILTER, NOT A PER-DEVICE FILTER. This is the single most important capture setting. The device re-enumerates from PID 0x1978 to PID 0x1977 and back, taking a new USB address each time. USBPcap must be started on the whole root hub the mouse is attached to, with 'Capture from all devices connected to selected roothubs' enabled and 'Inject already connected devices descriptors' on. A capture filtered to one device address loses the entire bootloader phase — which is exactly where every firmware byte goes.
- CAPTURE CONTROL TRANSFERS. Feature reports travel on the control pipe, not an interrupt endpoint. Any filter or display setting that hides control transfers hides 100% of the firmware payload.
- NO SNAP LENGTH. The A0 06 data stage is 1041 bytes. Set USBPcap's snap length to unlimited and leave Wireshark's 'Limit each packet to N bytes' unchecked, or every payload is truncated and none of the hashes can be computed.
- START THE CAPTURE BEFORE LAUNCHING THE UPDATER and keep it running well past the success message. The resource load, the initial enumeration, the A1 3A frames, both re-enumerations and the trailing A1 13 all have to be inside the file.
- PLUG THE MOUSE INTO A PORT ON THE CAPTURED ROOT HUB and do not move it. Do not unplug or replug at any point during the run.
- CAPTURE THE CONFIG BLOB BEFORE AND AFTER. Run the Configuration Tool once and capture the A1 12 request plus the 1041-byte A0 GetFeature response BEFORE the flash, then again after. This is the only way to answer whether the flash and/or the trailing A1 13 disturbs settings, and the 'before' half exists exactly once.
- DO NOT CHANGE ANY SETTING in the Configuration Tool during the 'before' visit — read-only. A write would alter the very blob we are trying to preserve as the reference.
- IF POSSIBLE RUN THE UPDATER TWICE AND CAPTURE BOTH. The tool does not gate on version (fw110 only displays it), so a second 1.10-onto-1.10 flash is expected to work and gives a clean second capture to diff. Do the second run only after reviewing the first.
- EXPORT FOR COMPARISON: from the finished capture, extract every SET_REPORT whose data stage begins A0 06, concatenate bytes [16..1039] of each in capture order, and hash. That single number is the whole test — it must be 8148ebe9f8d2848abe483aee98df6e42bab341c6a17523bfef0f85f1f66754d0 for updater 1.10.
- RECORD THE SIDE FACTS A CAPTURE CANNOT SHOW: whether the mouse was ever unplugged or a button held; how many times it disconnected and roughly when; total elapsed wall time; what the progress bar did; the version number shown before and after; and whether anything failed or needed a retry.

### Config 1.07 — startup, device open, reading the settings record

- Start the USBPcap capture BEFORE the mouse is plugged in. The plug-in enumeration (GET_DESCRIPTOR device/config/string, GET_DESCRIPTOR REPORT, SET_CONFIGURATION, SET_IDLE) is the cross-check on notes/device-predictions.md's 156-byte and 69-byte report descriptors and costs nothing to include.
- Capture the whole root hub the mouse is on, not a filtered subset. A filter set from a guess about the interface number would silently drop the very traffic these predictions are about.
- Volume warning: interface B is a mouse at up to 8000 Hz, and every movement is an interrupt-IN URB. Set the mouse down and do not move or click it, from before plug-in until after the tool is closed. Drive the tool with the keyboard or a second mouse. If the file is still unwieldy, keep it anyway — the four control transfers filter out trivially in Wireshark with `usb.bmRequestType == 0x21 || usb.bmRequestType == 0xa1`.
- Run configuration tool version 1.07 specifically, matching the SHA-256 in notes/binaries.md. Every address in this document is cfg107; if a different build is on the laptop, say so before the capture is interpreted.
- Sequence exactly: (1) start capture, (2) plug in the mouse, (3) wait about 10 s for Windows to settle, (4) launch the config tool, (5) wait for the UI to finish populating, (6) touch nothing at all, (7) close the window with the title-bar X, (8) stop the capture.
- BEFORE closing the window, photograph or screenshot ALL FOUR tabs — Basic, Advanced Sensor, Buttons, Button Mapping — plus the Firmware Version and Software Version readouts on the main window. Reading the record without the matching UI state wastes most of the capture's value: the pairing is what turns located offsets into named fields.
- Also record explicitly: whether the LOD combo has 12 entries or 2 (that reads out record byte 0x6F), whether the Polling Rate combo is enabled or greyed out, which of the four unlabelled radio buttons is selected, and the exact number in every CPI and X/Y edit box.
- Strongly recommended companion: run Process Monitor filtered to the config tool's process, with CreateFile and DeviceIoControl operations enabled, over the same window. That is the only way to test the enumeration predictions — USBPcap cannot see SetupAPI or HID-class IOCTLs. Save the ProcMon log next to the pcapng.
- Save the pcapng uncompressed and complete. Do not pre-filter and re-save; a prediction that fails because the evidence was filtered out teaches nothing.
- If a second run is cheap, repeat the identical sequence. Two matching captures establish that the four-transfer sequence is deterministic — one of the six invariants CLAUDE.md §4.3 asks for, and one that cannot be established from a single run.

### Config 1.07 — writing settings, and the differential experiment

- TOOL: 'Endgame Gear OP1 8k v2 Configuration Tool v1.07.exe' ONLY. sha256 must match notes/binaries.md. Do NOT run 1.00, 1.01 or 1.04 in the same capture — cfg100 is a separate code base with a different record builder (0x004050e0) and would contaminate the diff. Do NOT run any firmware updater.
- FILTER: capture the mouse's device only, not the whole root hub. The mouse presents TWO USB HID interfaces; the vendor collections are on the one whose report descriptor is 156 bytes (the other is the 69-byte plain mouse). Capture BOTH interfaces if USBPcap will only do whole-device — but DO NOT MOVE THE MOUSE during the capture: at 8000 Hz the plain-mouse interface will bury everything. Use the keyboard (Tab/arrows/Enter) to drive the UI wherever possible; where a click is unavoidable, move, pause 2 s, then click.
- START THE CAPTURE BEFORE PLUGGING THE MOUSE IN, so the enumeration (device, configuration and both report descriptors) is in the file. Then wait 10 s before launching the tool.
- STEP 0 — BASELINE, do this before touching any setting. Launch the tool, wait 10 s, do nothing. Expect exactly four control transfers: SET 0xA1/0x02 (64) -> GET 0xA1 (64) -> SET 0xA1/0x12 (64) -> GET 0xA0 (1041). SAVE THE 1041-BYTE RESPONSE — it is the device's untouched record and the reference for every later diff, and once Factory Reset runs it is gone forever.
- STEP 1 — Polling Rate. Tab 'Basic' (page 135), combo control 1085 at the top left under the 'Polling Rate' label. Set it to '125Hz', press APPLY. PREDICT: one A0 11; report byte 0x15 becomes 0x40; nothing else changes.
- STEP 2 — Polling Rate '125Hz' -> '8000Hz', APPLY. PREDICT report byte 0x15 = 0x01.
- STEP 3 — Polling Rate '8000Hz' -> '4000Hz', APPLY. PREDICT report byte 0x15 = 0x02. (Steps 2 and 3 together test the one place the one-hot table is NOT monotonic: index 5 -> 0x02 and index 6 -> 0x01.) Then set it back to '1000Hz' and APPLY (report byte 0x15 = 0x08).
- STEP 4 — LOD, the rival-encoding test. Combo 1024, immediately below Polling Rate, next to the 'LOD' label. Select '1.7mm', press APPLY. PREDICT report byte 0x19 = 0x0A. RIVAL: 0xD9. Nothing else changes. This single byte decides whether my reading of the APPLY sequence (harvest runs, then pack) is right at all.
- STEP 5 — LOD -> '0.7mm', APPLY. PREDICT report byte 0x19 = 0x00 (rival 0xC2).
- STEP 6 — LOD -> '2.0mm', APPLY. PREDICT report byte 0x19 = 0x03, i.e. the SAME byte '1.0mm' produces. Then LOD -> '1.0mm', APPLY, and confirm the byte does not change (no A0 11 at all is also an acceptable outcome here, since the dirty-check would see no difference). Restore LOD to its original value.
- STEP 7 — Angle Snapping, checkbox 1064 (top middle, 'Angle Snapping'). Tick it, APPLY. PREDICT report byte 0x1A: 0x00 -> 0x01. Untick, APPLY, back to 0x00.
- STEP 8 — Disable LED on Lift-Off, checkbox 1066 (top right). Tick it, APPLY. PREDICT report byte 0x18: 0x01 -> 0x00 (INVERTED — this is the point of the step).
- STEP 9 — CPI Levels, combo 1019 (top centre-right, under 'CPI Levels'). Set to '2', APPLY. PREDICT report byte 0x1E: 0x04 -> 0x02. Set back to '4', APPLY.
- STEP 10 — CPI stage radio, LIVE APPLY, DO NOT PRESS APPLY. Click the small unlabelled radio button at the far left of the 'CPI 2' row (control 1067, dialog units x=8 y=132). PREDICT: exactly one A0 11 appears within ~300 ms of the click, with no APPLY press; report byte 0x1D = 0x01. Wait 3 s.
- STEP 11 — Click the radio on the 'CPI 4' row (control 1069, x=8 y=241). PREDICT one A0 11, report byte 0x1D = 0x03. Then click the 'CPI 1' radio (1062): report byte 0x1D = 0x00.
- STEP 12 — Confirm the negative: click into the 'CPI 1' edit box (control 1020, right-hand end of the CPI 1 row), type 1600 over the existing value, wait 5 s. PREDICT: ZERO USB traffic. Then press APPLY. PREDICT one A0 11 with report bytes 0x34,0x35 = 40 06.
- STEP 13 — Quantiser. Type 806 into the CPI 1 edit, APPLY. PREDICT report bytes 0x34,0x35 = 2A 03 (810). Type 12345, APPLY. PREDICT 3E 30 (12350). Type 5, APPLY. PREDICT 0A 00 (10). Type 40000, APPLY. PREDICT 30 75 (30000). Then set CPI 1 back to 400.
- STEP 14 — THE CPI-4 FLAG BUG. Tick 'X/Y Settings' (checkbox 1063) so the Y edits are usable. Set CPI 3 to X=800, Y=1600 (different). Set CPI 4 to X=3200, Y=3200 (equal). Press APPLY. PREDICT report byte 0x3D (CPI 3 flag) = 0x01 AND report byte 0x42 (CPI 4 flag) = 0x01 even though CPI 4's X equals its Y. Then, WITHOUT changing anything, click the CPI 4 radio (1069) and watch the live-apply frame: PREDICT report byte 0x42 = 0x00 in that frame. Two frames, one byte, opposite values — that is the whole test.
- STEP 15 — Untick 'X/Y Settings', APPLY. PREDICT all four Y values become equal to their X values and report bytes 0x33, 0x38, 0x3D, 0x42 all become 0x00.
- STEP 16 — Advanced Sensor tab (page 140). Tick 'Motion Sync' (1028), APPLY. PREDICT report byte 0x1C: 0x00 -> 0x01.
- STEP 17 — Tick 'Motion Jitter Filter' (1030), APPLY. PREDICT report byte 0x16 changes by exactly +0x10 and NO other byte changes (this is the packed-byte test).
- STEP 18 — Tick 'Force max Sensor fps' (1032), APPLY. PREDICT report byte 0x81: 0x00 -> 0x01.
- STEP 19 — 'Smoothing Tuning' combo (1084): select each of its three items in turn, APPLY after each. PREDICT the LOW nibble of report byte 0x1B goes 2 (item 'Force Off'), 0 ('Ripple Control Off'), 1 ('Ripple Control On'), with the high nibble unchanged throughout.
- STEP 20 — 'CPI Downshift Tuning' combo (1082): select each of its FOUR run-time items in turn ('Force Off', 'Light Timer Only', 'Medium Timer Only', 'Default'), APPLY after each. PREDICT the HIGH nibble of report byte 0x1B goes 0x8, 0xC, 0x4, 0x0, low nibble unchanged. Also record the exact item list the combo shows, since the resource DLGINIT lists only three and the code fills four.
- STEP 21 — 'Sensor Angle Tuning' trackbar (1038): drag it to its minimum, APPLY; then maximum, APPLY. PREDICT report byte 0x80 takes the two end-of-range values and record what they are — the range itself is not derived.
- STEP 22 — 'Sensor Glass Mode' (1031): tick it, APPLY. I have NO prediction for this one: it is a page-140 control that the APPLY harvest 0x411ab0 does not read. Whether it moves any byte at all is exactly what the step is for.
- STEP 23 — Button Mapping tab (page 153). Drag 'Left Button Multiclick Filter' (trackbar 1066) to a distinctive position, APPLY. PREDICT report byte 0x4D takes the raw slider position and no other byte changes.
- STEP 24 — Left 'SPDT:' combo (1062) -> 'GX Speed Mode', APPLY. PREDICT report byte 0x4D = 0xF1. -> 'GX Safe Mode', APPLY. PREDICT 0xF0. -> 'OFF', APPLY. PREDICT it returns to the slider value from step 23.
- STEP 25 — Tick 'Slamclick Filter' (1029) on the same page, APPLY. PREDICT report byte 0x16 changes by exactly bit 0 only. Tick the long acknowledgement checkbox (1064), APPLY. PREDICT report byte 0x82: 0x00 -> 0x01.
- STEP 26 — Buttons tab (page 139). Reassign 'MIDDLE CLICK' to something else, APPLY. PREDICT the change lands inside report bytes 0x51..0x57 (the third 7-byte record). Record what the two leading bytes become for each assignment tried — the type/code encoding beyond the defaults is not derived.
- STEP 27 — LAST, AND ONLY LAST. Press 'Factory Reset' (main window, top right, next to the version readout). PREDICT: exactly one SET 0xA1/0x13 (64 bytes, A1 13 then 62 zeros), a 1100 ms gap, one GET 0xA1 (64 bytes). NO A0 11 and NO A1 12. The APPLY button greys out.
- STEP 28 — Immediately after the reset, without restarting: change one setting (e.g. tick Angle Snapping) to re-enable APPLY, then press APPLY. PREDICT the full default record given in prediction 'factory-reset-then-apply-record', with report bytes 0x1F..0x32 STILL EQUAL to the STEP 0 baseline read rather than to anything the reset produced.
- STEP 29 — Quit the tool completely, then relaunch it. PREDICT another four-transfer start-up. SAVE this 1041-byte response too: it is the device's true post-reset record, and comparing it against the STEP 28 frame is the only way to see what the device's factory reset actually did versus what the host thinks it did.
- REPORTING: for every step, note (a) the number of SET/GET control transfers, (b) the full 1041-byte SET data as hex, (c) the timestamp deltas between SET and the following GET. A per-step hex dump of report bytes 0x10..0x82 is enough for most steps, but keep the whole frames — the claim that bytes 0x83..0x410 are always zero is one of the predictions.
- ALSO NOTE, per step, whether Windows shows any UAC/driver prompt and whether the tool ever reports an error dialog — a failed write shows up as three identical A0 11 frames at 300/350/400 ms and I want to know if that ever happened.

### Unsolicited traffic, the event channel, the third collection

- Capture at the USB level with USBPcap bound to the ROOT HUB or the whole device, not filtered to one endpoint and not taken from a HID handle. Every prediction here about report 0x08 depends on seeing transfers for collections no application has open.
- Start the capture BEFORE plugging the mouse in. Enumeration (GET_DESCRIPTOR device/config/string, both GET_DESCRIPTOR(Report) responses, SET_CONFIGURATION, SET_IDLE) and the attach-time input reports are only visible to a capture that was already running. Unplug/replug is repeatable, so this is recoverable — but only if noticed.
- Keep a written, wall-clock action log and align it to capture timestamps. An unannotated capture of a mouse being used cannot distinguish 'the device volunteered this' from 'the user did something'. The most valuable segment is the stretch where the log says NOTHING WAS TOUCHED.
- Segment 1, mandatory: 15 minutes with the mouse plugged in, on the desk, untouched, with NO vendor software running. This is the only test of event-not-periodic-when-idle and the cleanest window for report 0x08.
- Segment 2: isolated physical actions with at least 10 s of silence between each, logged individually — one left click; one right click; one middle click; each side button separately (record which is physically which); three wheel detents up; three down; a slow 2 s movement; a fast flick; lift the mouse and set it down; then every on-board control, one press at a time, cycling all the way round.
- Segment 3: start the vendor config tool with the capture still running; read every page without changing anything; change ONE setting (the polling rate) and APPLY; change it back and APPLY; quit the tool. The polling-rate change tests event-echo-after-apply, event-crosscheck-polling-rate-blob and rate-change-no-reenumeration in one go.
- Segment 4, a separate capture: bootloader entry, a full firmware update, and the return to application mode. This is the only environment in which report 0x08 has never been looked for, and [O] shows the collection exists there too.
- Verify the positive control before drawing any negative conclusion: the capture must contain at least one report-0x03 frame. Without it, an absence of report 0x08 says nothing about the device.
- Movement at 8000 Hz produces 8000 packets per second. Keep movement segments to a few seconds each or the file size buries the interesting parts. Do NOT solve this by lowering the polling rate before segment 1 — segment 1 must be at whatever the device ships with.
- Change no setting before segments 1 and 2. The default state of this unit is itself an observation and the first APPLY destroys it.
- Record the exact bytes of both GET_DESCRIPTOR(Report) responses and diff them against .analysis/hidobserve_3367.json and .analysis/ioreg_bootloader_1977_20260904.txt. Any difference means the Windows machine is not looking at the device we characterised.
- If a Windows-side HID read on the FF02/0x02 collection is attempted to solicit report 0x08, it must be a READ ONLY. Send nothing to that collection: nothing is known about it and CLAUDE.md §1.3 forbids writing a byte whose meaning is [G].

### What the vendor software looks like on screen

- Record which exact files are being run, by SHA-256, before anything else. The predictions above are for 'Endgame Gear OP1 8k v2 Configuration Tool v1.07.exe' and 'Endgame Gear OP1 8k v2 Firmware Updater 1.10.exe' as they exist in this repo (notes/binaries.md carries the hashes). Running 1.04 or 1.01 of the config tool invalidates the tab predictions outright: cfg104 has no DIALOG 153 at all and its DIALOG 140 carries 31 controls instead of 11.
- CONFIG TOOL FIRST, and take all of it before any flash. The updater's success path sends A1 13 (factory reset), so every config screenshot taken after a flash is of a wiped device.
- Config tool, with the mouse connected, at 100% display scaling: (1) the whole window on the Basic tab; (2) each of the other three tabs; (3) each of these combos DROPPED OPEN so every item is visible — CPI Levels, LOD, Polling Rate, CPI Downshift Tuning, Smoothing Tuning, and both SPDT combos; (4) one of the six Button Mapping buttons clicked so the popup menu is open, plus each of its three submenus open in turn.
- Config tool, the negative checks: right-click the title bar (system menu), right-click the client area, press Alt then each letter, press F1, resize the window larger than default and screenshot. None of these should produce a menu, an About box, an LED page, or the DIALOG 131 update window. Also scroll/look at the bottom of the Basic page for 'Apply CPI settings' and 'Surface Calibration'.
- Config tool, the APPLY state test: screenshot APPLY at startup (predicted greyed), then tick 'Angle Snapping' and screenshot again (predicted enabled), then untick it and screenshot (predicted greyed again).
- Config tool, the live-write test with USB capture running: click one of the four CPI-stage radio buttons on the Basic page and confirm traffic appears immediately with APPLY never being pressed. Then tick a plain checkbox and confirm NO traffic appears until APPLY is pressed. This distinguishes the live-apply path (0x00414010) from the deferred one.
- Updater, before any flash and costing nothing: unplug the mouse, launch the updater, screenshot the idle window, click 'Update Firmware', screenshot the 'Device not found' state, then plug the mouse back in and click Update twice more, screenshotting each time. The prediction is that nothing happens on those later clicks. Then close and relaunch the updater before doing anything real.
- Updater, the real run — record it as VIDEO, not stills. The status box and progress bar change in a fixed order and some states last well under a second. A screen recording at >= 15 fps with the USB capture running in parallel is the only way to get the ordering and the timing. Note the wall-clock time from click to success text.
- USB capture filter: capture the whole root hub the mouse is on, not a single device, because the mouse changes USB address and PID (0x1978 -> 0x1977 -> 0x1978) twice during an update, and a per-device filter loses the bootloader phase entirely. Start the capture BEFORE launching either tool.
- Do NOT press 'Factory Reset' in the config tool until the settings blob has been captured (the A1 12 read plus its 1041-byte GetFeature response), and not before the config screenshots are complete.
- Screenshot the Details tab of each .exe's file properties, for the FileVersion / CompanyName / OriginalFilename prediction.
- Note whether the four tab captions show visible extra padding (they contain two leading and two trailing spaces) and whether they render as buttons or as classic notebook tabs — the whole tab-control style reading depends on it.
